Simulation of Separable Quantum Measurements on Bipartite States via Likelihood POVMs
Arun Padakandla

Abstract
By developing a new framework of likelihood POVMs, analysis techniques and a new proof of the quantum covering lemma, we address the simulation of separable quantum measurement over bipartite states. In addition to a new one shot inner bound that naturally generalizes to the asymptotic case, we demonstrate the power, generality and universality of the developed techniques in the most general distributed measurement scenario by recovering all current known inner bounds. In addition to the above results, this framework is appealing in being the most natural and simple POVM simulation protocol.



I
Introduction

Measurements play a central role in both quantum information processing and quantum physics. The outcome of a measurement being inherently random, a question of fundamental interest
[
1
,
2
]
is to quantify the amount of
information
it contains about the quantum state being measured. Building on a series of works
[
3
,
4
]
and adopting an information-theoretic approach, Winter formulated
[
5
]
this question into what is popularly referred to as the measurement compression problem (MCP). Solving the same,
[
5
]
provided a definitive answer that is both mathematically rigorous and intuitively satisfying. Subsequently, the MCP has garnered considerable attention and revealed connections to several other problems. In this work, we develop a unified framework of tools and analysis techniques to solve the MCP in diverse network measurement scenarios and thereby derive new inner bounds.

Winter’s elegant formulation and solution of the MCP provide a profound insight on the measurement process. In addition to being inherently fundamental, at the heart of the MCP is the quantum classical (QC) covering
[
6
]
, a notion central to several important problems
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. Indeed, QC covering plays a key role in private communication over quantum channels, reverse Shannon theorems, among others. These connections have motivated MCP studies in diverse network scenarios
[
2
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
and fostered connections to several problems. Interests go beyond conventional asymptotic studies
[
5
,
2
]
. Indeed, the MCP has been studied
[
15
,
13
]
in the one-shot regime wherein a single realization of the quantum state and measurement are permitted. In contrast to the former, one-shot studies
[
16
,
17
]
are more constrained in terms of strategies, analysis tools and are therefore considered to be more general and challenging. For example, simplifying primitives such as time-sharing and measure-concentration based tools are outside its purview. This has led to the development of novel ideas
[
18
]
, measures
[
18
]
and more general tools
[
19
]
such as convex split
[
15
,
20
,
21
]
- one that we will employ in this work.

Despite these important developments, we lack a
simple unified
set of tools and techniques that can solve the MCP in diverse network measurement scenarios in the asymptotic and one-shot regimes. This is evident as soon as one considers MCP beyond the original scenario
[
5
]
that involves one measurement on a single state (Fig.
2
) - referred to henceforth as the
pint-to-point (PTP) scenario
. While Anshu, Jain and Warsi
[
15
]
develop techniques for the PTP MCP in the one-shot regime, these require certain resources in a catalytically and moreover are sub-optimal for the MCP with quantum side information (MCP-QSI) scenario. Addressing these deficiencies,
[
13
]
solve the MCP-QSI in the one-shot regime. However, the latter techniques
[
13
]
are quite different from those used in the asymptotic setting
[
5
,
2
,
12
]
. This lack of a unified tool set comes to a head when we consider the next generalization - the
distributed MCP
(Fig.
2
)
scenario
wherein the goal is to quantify the information content in the outcome of a separable measurement on a bipartite distributed state. Adopting the involved techniques of
[
5
]
,
[
12
]
attempt to derive bounds for the one-shot setting, but is only able to do so for very restricted instances. Consequently, the best known inner bound for the distributed MCP of Fig.
2
in the one-shot regime is not the one-shot counterpart of the inner bound proved achievable in the asymptotic regime. In the same vein, it must also be noted that the techniques
[
5
,
2
]
, novel while they were, are quite involved. Surprisingly, these same techniques were adopted in
[
22
,
14
,
23
,
12
]
, indicating the lack of simplified tool set to solve the MCP even in the asymptotic regime.

In this article, we develop a new simulation POVM (positive operator valued measurement) - a key tool in the solution to the MCP - based on
likelihood POVMs
(Sec.
III
). While the latter are the most natural to solve the MCP, owing to its structure its analysis has remained elusive. This has forced the use of highly complicated POVMs (Rem.
1
) in
[
5
,
2
,
22
,
14
,
23
,
12
]
. Developing the idea of proxy states and new techniques to analyze its performance, we present here a
unified framework
that solves the MCP in
diverse network scenarios
. In this preprint, we focus on the
distributed MCP scenario
(Fig.
2
) and our unified framework enables us study both the one-shot and asymptotic regimes using both random IID (Sec.
IV-A
) and random structured codebook (Sec.
IV-B
) based POVMs. As we elaborate in the sequel, we derive
new inner bounds
(Thm.
3
) and recover current known best (Thms.
4
and
5
) for the distributed MCP. Our framework neither relies on time sharing nor does it require catalytic use of any resources. We refer the reader to
[
13
]
where we address the
centralized MCP
with
distributed Rxs
using this same framework of likelihood POVMs and analysis techniques.

Fig. 1:
The components in the two blue ellipses must be statistically indistinguishable.

Fig. 2:
Txs
j
, holding system
j
of bipartite state
\rho^{12}
shares (i) common randomness at rate
C_{j}
bits/measurement (msmt) and (ii) noiseless bit pipe of rate
R_{j}
bits/msmt, both with Rx. The goal is to simulate separable POVM
\lambda
.

Our focus in this preprint is on the distributed MCP scenario in its full generality (Fig.
2
), wherein the outcome of a
separable measurement
1
1

1

A
separable measurement
is one that can be simulated via separate measurements on the two components followed by (joint) stochastic processing of the outcomes. For a formal definition, see Defn.
3
.

\lambda
on a bipartite state
\rho^{12}
has to be simulated at a central Rx. Owing to the separation of the two components and the associated Txs, the distributed MCP scenario is, as discussed below, richer and more challenging than the PTP MCP. First, we consider simulation via POVMs based on IID random codebooks in Sec.
IV-A
. Enriching the likelihood POVMs and the associated techniques developed in Sec.
III
to solve the MCP in the PTP scenario, we derive a new inner bound (Thm.
3
) to the distributed MCP (Fig.
2
) in the one-shot regime. In addition to strictly improving the previously known best one-shot inner bound
[
12
, Thm. 3]
these same proof techniques enables us recover the best known inner bound (Thm.
4
) in the asymptotic regime. Alternatively stated, our unified framework is able to achieve an inner bound in the one-shot regime corresponding to the best known inner bound in the asymptotic regime - a finding not proven thus far.

In contrast to conventional/widely-held belief, the technique of solving the MCP via
unstructured POVMs
, i.e. simulation POVMs based on independent and identically distributed (IID) random codebooks is in general sub-optimal. In particular, if the separable POVM to be simulated has a ‘certain coupled’ structure, then simulation via
structured POVMs
, i.e POVMs based on
jointly designed codes possessing inter- and intra structural properties
, specifically certain algebraic (closure) properties, can yield strictly smaller inner bounds in the asymptotic regime. To elaborate, if the codewords of the two random codes are IID and binned independently, then simulation via corresponding POVMs might require strictly larger rates in comparison to a simulation via structured POVMs based on random codebooks possessing
joint algebraic closure
properties. This can be traced back to the ingenious work of Körner and Marton
[
24
]
in the context of mod-2 sum recovery - an instance of distributed classical covering - and subsequent works
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
. These have led to the use of structured POVMs for MCP
[
14
]
, where it has been proven
[
14
, Rem. 1, Ex. 1,2]
that these can outperform unstructured POVMs. The need for richer code structures unequivocally portrays the fact that the distributed scenario facilitates a richer setting to study MCP.

While random structured codes - random jointly designed codes with algebraic properties - can yield strictly better inner bounds in multiple network scenarios
[
26
]
, it is more involved. Indeed, the distribution of jointly designed random codes with inter and intra- algebraic properties do not possess simplifying properties such as mutual independence etc., that IID random codes enjoy, lending the former’s analysis more involved. For instance, the operator Chernoff bound (OCB) of Ahlswede Winter
[
28
]
which is often used to analyze performance in MCP, relies on mutual independence and cannot be employed to analyze structured POVMs. It must also be noted that while we are able to achieve better inner bounds using structured codes in the asymptotic regime, there have been no studies aimed at achieving corresponding inner bounds in the one-shot regime. These point to the challenges of deriving inner bounds via structured codes. Notwithstanding these, we are able to derive the current best known inner bound (Thm.
5
in Sec.
IV-B
) via structured POVMs via the same technique developed for unstructured POVMs by appropriately modifying the underlying codes and decoding strategy. This validates the power and universality of our likelihood POVM based framework.

Following notation, we motivate, describe and formulate the MCP in Sec.
II
. In Sec.
III
, we describe the simplicity of the likelihood POVMs and demonstrate the techniques we have developed by solving the MCP in the PTP scenario. As we discuss in Sec.
III-B
, the distributed MCP, and in particular the idea of identifying proxy states for analysis of likelihood POVMs is more involved for general entangled bipartite states. In Sec.
IV-A
, we enhance the proxy states idea and derive a new inner bound (Thm.
3
) to the distributed MCP in the one-shot regime. The power of the ideas developed in proof of Thm.
3
is evident from the fact that the same proof holds verbatim for the asymptotic regime with both unstructured and structured code based likelihood POVMs. In regards to the final QC covering bounds, we leverage the standard
[
29
]
and the tripartite convex split lemmas
[
30
]
. In addition to these, we also provide a proof of the conventional QCL in the asymptotic regime that is much simpler than the operator Chernoff bound of Ahlswede and Winter
[
28
]
.

The focus of this version of our preprint is to (i) provide complete proofs of Thms.
3
,
4
and
5
. Moreover, we intend to portray a unified proof technique that serves to prove all Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
with minimal modifications.
The proof of Thm
3
we provide in Sec.
IV-A
achieves this. Firstly, proof of Thm.
3
holds verbatim as a proof for Thm.
4
. The only change required is an appropriate substitution of the final covering bounds. See Rem.
3
,
6
. Next, the proof of Thm.
3
holds verbatim as a proof for Thm.
5
too with two natural modifications. First, the IID codebooks must be replaced via jointly designed coset codes. Next, the decoding POVM must be modified. With these alterations in place, the proof of Thm.
3
can be ‘plugged and played’ to prove Thm.
5
. We elaborate on these elements in Remarks
3
,
6
. In a subsequent version of this preprint, we study the centralized MCP scenario.

II
Preliminaries; MCP Description, Statement and Results

Notation
: We supplement standard notation
[
6
]
adopted in quantum information theory with the following. For a positive integer
n
, we let
[n]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{1,\cdots,n\},[\overline{n}]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{0\}\cup[n]
. All Hilbert (state) spaces are assumed to be finite dimensional.
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
denote the collection of linear, Hermitian, positive and density operators acting on Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
respectively.
\alpha^{*}\in\mathbb{C}
and
T^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
denote complex conjugate of
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and adjoint of
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
respectively.
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
will denote state space of the accessible component of our quantum systems and
\mathcal{H}_{X},\mathcal{H}_{\underline{X}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{X_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{X_{2}}
will denote the reference space or the state space of the inaccessible components. Having said this, we often ignore the subscript
A
in
\mathcal{H}_{A}
of the accessible state space to reduce clutter. For state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
with spectral decomposition
\rho=\sum_{x}\alpha_{x}\outerproduct{e_{x}}{e_{x}}
,
\ket{\phi_{\rho}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A}
denotes a purification,
\ket{\varphi_{\rho}}=\sum_{x}\sqrt{\alpha_{x}}\ket{e_{x}}\otimes\ket{e_{x}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A}
denotes the
canonical purification
. In the latter case,
\mathcal{H}_{X}=\mathcal{H}_{A}
are isomorphic. We let
\phi_{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho}}{\phi_{\rho}}
and
\varphi_{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\outerproduct{\varphi_{\rho}}{\varphi_{\rho}}
.
\norm{A}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\tr(\sqrt{AA^{\dagger}})
denotes trace norm,
F(\rho,\sigma)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\norm{\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\sigma}}_{1}
denotes fidelity between states
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
P(\rho,\sigma)=\sqrt{1-\left[F(\rho,\sigma)\right]^{2}}
denotes purified distance.

Positive Operator Valued Measurements (POVMs) - a collection of positive operators summing to the identity - play a central role in this article. We refer to the collection
\{\lambda_{y}:y\in\mathcal{Y}\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
forming a POVM with outcome set
\mathcal{Y}
either as
\lambda
or often times as
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Associated with POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mbox{span}\{\ket{y}:y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
with
\innerproduct{\haty}{y}=\delta_{\hat{y}y}
and CPTP maps
{\overline{\mathscr{E}}}^{\lambda}(\cdot),\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}(\cdot)
defined as
\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{\lambda}(s)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}s\sqrt{\lambda_{y}}\otimes\outerproduct{y}{y}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}(s)=\sum_{y}\tr(\lambda_{y}s)\outerproduct{y}{y}
. Analogously, for a stochastic matrix
(p_{Y|W}(y|w):(w,y)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Y})
, we let
\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{{Y|W}}_{p}(\cdot),\mathscr{E}^{{Y|W}}_{p}(\cdot)
denote the CPTP maps
\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{{Y|W}}_{p}(a)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{(w,y)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Y}}p_{Y|W}(y|w)\ket{w}\!\matrixelement{w}{a}{w}\!\bra{w}\otimes\outerproduct{y}{y}
and
\mathscr{E}^{{Y|W}}_{p}(a)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{(w,y)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Y}}p_{Y|W}(y|w)\matrixelement{w}{a}{w}\outerproduct{y}{y}
.

Illustrates original and simulated POVMs. The components in the two blue ellipses must be statistically indistinguishable.

In the asymptotic regime, we shall regularly deal with
n-
length/tensor product objects. We employ a
bold
notation to represent appropriate aggregation. For state space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, set
\mathcal{Y}
, POVM
\lambda
or
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
, we let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{A}^{\otimes n}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{Y}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\lambda_{y_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\lambda_{y^{n}}:\boldsymbol{y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(y_{1},\cdots,y^{n})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{Y}^{n}\}
and
\boldsymbol{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho^{\otimes n}
respectively.
In addition when
c=(\boldsymbol{y}(k,m)=(y_{1}(k,m),\cdots,y_{n}(k,m)):(k,m)\in[K]\times[M])
is code, i.e. a collection,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{k,m}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\lambda_{y_{t}(k,m)}
.

Our rates in the asymptotic regime will be characterized in terms of Von Neumonn entropies and corresponding mutual information quantities. For a state
\sigma_{XY}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y})
, we let
I(X;Y)_{\sigma}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}H(\sigma_{X})+H(\sigma_{Y})-H(\sigma_{XY})
where
\sigma_{X}=\tr_{Y}(\sigma_{XY}),\sigma_{Y}=\tr_{X}(\sigma_{XY})
and
H(\rho)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}-\tr(\rho\log\rho)
denotes the Von Neumonn entropy. In the one-shot regime, our rates are characterized via smooth entropies
[
18
,
19
,
29
,
31
,
32
]
. Specifically, for any
\epsilon>0
and
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
, let
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(\rho)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\tilde{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}):P(\rho,\tilde{\rho})\}\leq\epsilon
. For
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying
\mbox{supp}(\rho)\subseteq\mbox{supp}(\sigma)
, we define the max-relative entropy as
\mathcal{D}_{\max}(\rho||\sigma)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\inf\{r\in\mathbb{R}:\rho\leq 2^{r}\sigma\}
. For
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying
\mbox{supp}(\rho)\subseteq\mbox{supp}(\sigma)
and for
\epsilon>0
, we define the smooth max-relative entropy
\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\epsilon}(\rho||\sigma)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\inf\{\mathcal{D}_{\max}(\tilde{\rho}||\sigma):\tilde{\rho}\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(\rho)\}
. Finally, for
\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
\epsilon>0
, we define the hypothesis testing relative entropy
D_{H}^{\epsilon}(\rho||\sigma)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\inf\{-\log[\tr(\pi\sigma)]:0\leq\pi\leq I,\tr(\pi\rho)\geq 1-\epsilon\}
. We abbreviate “probability mass function”, “spectral decomposition” and “orthonormal basis” as PMF, SCD, ONB respectively.

Formulation & Statement of MCP
: Following
[
5
,
2
]
we describe formulation of the PTP MCP in the asymptotic regime and provide a precise problem statement in Defn.
1
. Therein we also precisely state one-shot the PTP MCP.
2
2

2

While one can recover the MCP statement in the asymptotic regime via the one-shot statement, we have chosen to provide the former only in the context of the PTP scenario to aid a reader not conversant with the MCP formulation.

When Alice performs POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
, a specific QC correlation is induced between the post-measurement state and her classical register that holds the outcome (Fig.
3
). What is the minimum number
R
of bits that Alice must communicate to Bob to induce the same correlation between the the post-measurement state she possesses and the contents of his classical register? Investigating this, Winter recognized that some fraction of the randomness in the outcome can be extrinsic to the quantum state and can possibly be sourced by an external random source statistically independent of the state. This led him to seek the design of a
POVM simulation protocol
employing an alternate
n-
letter measurement, which when executed on the tensor quantum state
\boldsymbol{\rho}
results in an identical post-measurement QC state as the original POVM
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}}
, but with the desirable property that the classical outcome of the protocol requires fewer bits to communicate. By communicating these fewer bits to Bob, the simulation protocol can potentially establish the same QC correlation we sought. We are thus led to the following.

Alice holding
n
copies of the state, i.e
\boldsymbol{\rho}
and an agnostic distributed observer Bob holding the classical register are provided
C
bits of
(external) shared randomness
per measurement (msmt). These
C
bits/msmt are statistically independent of the state
\boldsymbol{\rho}
. These can be utilized by Alice to potentially choose a POVM at random from a designed bank of POVMs. Alice can communicate
R
bits/msmt to Bob. Bob can then utilize the
nC
bits of shared randomness to post process the information he receives via the
nR
bits to populate his classical register. We require that the QC outcome of this protocol - the post-measurement state at Alice and Bob’s classical register contents - to be statistically indistinguishable from that of the original measurement
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}}
. Since the
C
shared random bits/msmt are statistically independent of the state
\rho
, Winter identified this as
extrinsic randomness
and the
R
bits/msmt communicated by Alice as the
intrinsic information
of the state
\rho
in the outcome of POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
. The minimum value of
R
for which, there exists a
C
and a corresponding POVM simulation protocol that can accomplish this, is therefore the information content in the outcome of the POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho
. Going further, the MCP seeks to characterize all such rate pairs
(R,C)
for which such a simulation protocol exists, thereby quantifying the entire trade-off between intrinsic information and extrinsic randomness in the outcome of POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on state
\rho
.

As we formalize below, a POVM simulation protocol comprises of a bank of
\boldsymbol{K}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}2^{nC}
POVMs
\{\theta_{k}:k\in[\boldsymbol{K}]\}
. Each POVM in this bank has at most
\boldsymbol{M}
outcomes, where
\boldsymbol{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}2^{nR}
. Alice measures the quantum state
\boldsymbol{\rho}
with the POVM indexed by the
nC
(external) shared random bits
k\in[\boldsymbol{K}]
and communicates the
nR
bits of the outcome
m\in[\boldsymbol{M}]
she observes (Fig.
2
). Bob having both the shared random bits
k\in[\boldsymbol{K}]
and now the outcome
m\in[\boldsymbol{M}]
, processes these via a corresponding bank
\Delta_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\Delta_{k,\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{M}}):\boldsymbol{y}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}\}
of decoder POVMs to simulate the classical outcome corresponding to the post-measurement state at Alice. Here
\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{M}}=\mbox{span}\{\ket{m}:m\in[\boldsymbol{M}]\}
with
\innerproduct{\hatm}{m}=\delta_{m\hat{m}}
. Assuming the shared random bits
k\in[\boldsymbol{K}]
are uniformly distributed, we identify the CPTP maps corresponding to the original POVM
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and simulation protocol as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{\boldsymbol{X}}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\}\mbox{ and }\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{\Delta,\theta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}\mbox{ where }\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{\boldsymbol{X}}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\Delta_{k}}\circ\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k}}\right\}\mbox{ respectively}.

(1)

We enforce the outcomes
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}(\varphi_{\rho})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\rho})
of the original POVM
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}}
and designed POVM simulation protocol respectively, be arbitrarily close. We are thus led to the following.

Definition 1
.

An
(n,K,M,\theta,\Delta)
(POVM) simulation protocol consists of a bank of
K
POVMs
\theta_{k}:k\in[K]
with each POVM
\theta_{k}=\{\theta_{k,m}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A}^{\otimes n}):m\in[M]\}
having at most
M
outcomes and a corresponding bank of
K
decoder POVMs
\Delta_{k}:k\in[K]
with
\Delta_{k}=\{\Delta_{k,y^{n}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{M}):y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\}
, wherein
\mathcal{Y}
is the outcome set of the POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
to be simulated.
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
can be perfectly simulated with (communication) cost
(R,C)
if
\forall\eta>0
, there exists
N_{\eta}
such that
\forall n\geq N_{\eta}
, there exists an
(n,K,M,\theta,\Delta)
satisfying
\frac{\log K}{n}\leq C+\eta,\frac{\log M}{n}\leq R+\eta
and
\norm{\left\{\boldCalO- \boldCalS_{\Delta,\theta}\right\}(\varphi_{\boldrho})}_{1}\leq\eta
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}(\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}),\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})
are defined through (
1
). We define

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{(R,C):\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\!\!\!\mbox{ 's action on state }\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\mbox{ can be perfectly simulated with cost }(R,C)\}.

A
(1,K,M,\theta,\Delta)
(POVM) simulation protocol is a
(K,M,\theta,\Delta)
one-shot (POVM) simulation protocol.
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
can be
\eta
-simulated in one-shot with (communication) cost
(R,C)
if there exists a
(K,M,\theta,\Delta)
one-shot POVM simulation protocol such that
\log K\leq C
,
\log M\leq R
and
\norm{\left\{\CalO- \CalS_{\Delta,\theta}\right\}(\varphi_{\rho})}_{1}\leq\eta
, where
\mathcal{O}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{X}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{X}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}\right\},\mathcal{S}_{\Delta,\theta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}\mbox{ where }\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{X}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\Delta_{k}}\circ\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k}}\right\}.\mbox{ We define }

(2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}(R,C):\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\mbox{'s action on }\rho_{\underline{X}}\mbox{ can be }\eta\mbox{-simulated with cost }(\underline{R},\underline{C})\\
\end{array}\!\!\right\}.

While in the asymptotic regime we seek to squeeze the tolerance parameter
\eta>0
arbitrarily via measure concentration, in the one-shot version we characterize rates as a function of
\eta
. The goal of the MCP in the asymptotic and one-shot regimes is to characterize
\mathscr{C}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
and
\mathscr{C}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
respectively.

Winter
[
5
]
derived an elegant computable characterization for
\mathscr{C}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
for the case when both Alice and Bob wish to possess outcome of the simulated POVM. In Thm.
1
, we state Wilde et. al.’s
[
2
]
generalization for the case when only Bob wishes to possess the simulated outcome. The PTP MCP in the one-shot regime was first studied by Anshu, Jain and Warsi in
[
29
]
who obtained the corresponding characterization for
\mathscr{C}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
stated in Thm.
2
.

Definition 2
.

For
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
and POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
, let
\mathcal{T}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
be all triples
(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})
satisfying (i)
\mathcal{W}
is a finite set, (ii)
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}=\{\mu_{w}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A}):w\in\mathcal{W}\}
is a POVM, and (iii)
(p_{Y|W}(y|w):(w,y)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Y})
is a stochastic matrix such that
\sum_{w\in\mathcal{Y}}p_{Y|W}(y|w)\mu_{w}=\lambda_{y}
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Corresponding to a triple
(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})
, let
\sigma_{XWY}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}\otimes{\overline{\mathscr{E}}}^{{Y|W}}_{p}\circ\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{\mu})(\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho}}{\phi_{\rho}})
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(\rho,\lambda)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{(R,C):R>I(X;W)_{\sigma},R+C>I(XY;W)_{\sigma}\mbox{ for some }(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})\in\mathcal{T}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})\}\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{(R,C):\begin{array}[]{c}\!\!\mbox{For some }(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})\in\mathcal{T}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}),~R>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XW}||\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2})\\
R+C>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XWY}||\sigma_{W}\otimes\sigma_{XY})+\log(4\eta^{-2})\end{array}\!\!\right\}

Theorem 1
.

POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on state
\rho
can be perfectly simulated at a cost
(R,C)
iff
(R,C)\in\mathscr{A}(\rho,\lambda)
.

Theorem 2
.

POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on state
\rho
can be
\eta
-simulated in one-shot with (communication) cost
(R,C)
if
(R,C)\in\mathscr{A}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
.

III
Likelihood POVMs and Solution to the MCP in the PTP Scenario

As is evident from Defns.
1
, the POVM simulation protocol, and in particular the design of the
K
encoder POVMs
\theta_{k}:k\in[K]
are central to solving the MCP. In addition to performing quantum covering for the MCP, these are also central in several other fundamental problems
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. To comprehend this design problem, let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
denote a state and
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\lambda_{y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
a POVM. A
n
-fold product measurement on
n-
fold tensor state
\boldsymbol{\rho}
results in post-measurement QC state

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{XY}^{n}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\!\sum_{y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\!\!\!\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\lambda_{y^{n}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\otimes\outerproduct{y^{n}}{y^{n}}\!.\mbox{ For large }n,\Upsilon_{XY}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}~\Gamma_{XY}^{n}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\!\sum_{y^{n}\in T_{\delta}(p_{Y})}\!\!\!2^{-nH(Y)}\!\!\left[2^{-nH(X|Y)}\Pi_{y^{n}}\!\right]\!\!\otimes\!\outerproduct{y^{n}}{y^{n}}

where the PMF
p_{Y}(y)=\tr(\lambda_{y}\rho)
,
\Pi_{y^{n}}
is a projector of dimension
2^{nH(X|Y)}
, where
H(\cdot),H(\cdot|\cdot)
are Von-Neumann entropies. The goal is to design an
n-
letter simulation POVM
\theta
which when performed on
\boldsymbol{\rho}
, results in a post-measurement state
\Gamma_{XY}^{n}
. Ideally, one would employ the operators
\theta_{y^{n}}=\frac{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\lambda_{y^{n}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{p_{Y}(y^{n})}\!:\!y^{n}\!\in\!T_{\delta}(p_{Y})
for the simulation POVM design. However,
\sum_{y^{n}\in T_{\delta}(p_{Y})}\theta_{y^{n}}
might dominate
I_{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}
.

3

Note the normalization by
p_{Y}^{n}(y^{n})
. This has led to ‘coating’

with several projectors - typical, conditional-typical, cut-off etc. This coating results in several difficulties, starting from its very definition

III-A

Likelihood POVMs
: Definition and Context

Given a collection (a codebook)
c_{k}=(y^{n}(m,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}:m\in[M])
, we define the
likelihood POVM

\theta_{k}=\{\theta_{k,m}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}):m\in[M]\}
with
M
outcomes as

\displaystyle\theta_{k,m}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{S_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\lambda_{y^{n}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}S_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!}{Mp_{Y}(y^{n})},\mbox{ where }S_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\in[M]}\!\!\!\frac{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\lambda_{y^{n}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{Mp_{Y}^{n}(y^{n})}.

(7)

Clearly,
\theta_{k}
is a POVM
6
6

6

with the appended operator
\theta_{k,-1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}I_{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}-\pi_{k}
where
\pi_{k}
is a projector on range space of
S_{k}
, if needed
and indeed the most natural from the above discussion. Though well known, its analysis for the MCP is very involved. Among others, one issue is that when we randomize over the codebook
c_{k}
, the presence of inverse term
S_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in Defn.
\theta_{k}
, now a random object, lends the likelihood POVMs not analyzable. It is for these analysis difficulties that despite awareness
7
7

7

As noted in
[
2
]
, the classical version of MCP is the channel synthesis problem​
[
33
]
and the above likelihood POVM is a quantum version of Cuff’s encoder​
[
33
]
. In spite of its simplicity,
[

,
12
]
, aware of
[
33
]
have not adopted the same.
and simplicity, the likelihood POVMs, to the best of the author’s knowledge have not been employed for the MCP.

A New Approach via a Proxy State
: Starting from
[
34
,
35
]
we have developed a new approach at analyzing the likelihood POVMs. Instead of analyzing the action of the likelihood POVMs on the given state
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
- a purification of
\boldsymbol{\rho}
- as is convention, we analyze the likelihood POVM’s action on a
proxy state
. Specifically, we analyze it on the canonical purification
\varphi_{S_{k}}
of
S_{k}
in (
7
). An informed reader recognizes that
S_{k}
approaches
\rho
. In fact, this yields one of our rate bounds. However, purifications of close states need not be close. Utilizing the freedom in purifications and the closeness of canonical purifications
[
5
, App. A]
, we developed​
[
34
,
35
]
a new approach to the MCP in the single terminal case.

III-B

Challenges in Distributed Scenario

To simulate a separable POVM in a distributed scenario, we need to design atleast 2 likelihood POVMs both acting on components of the same entangled state. Refer to. With limited freedom on purifications (owing to entanglement) and the availability of bounds on closeness of only canonical purifications, we now need
\varphi_{S_{k_{1}}}
and
\varphi_{S_{k_{2}}}
to be separately close to the same corresponding components of a single purification of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{X_{1}X_{2}}
, the latter possibly
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{12}}
. Next, the known results in regards to closeness of canonical purifications
[
5
, App. A]
relies on the two operators whose closeness we study, to be states, i.e. unit trace. Due to the two codebooks -
c_{1},c_{2}
in proof of Thm.
3
- being independently distributed -
S_{k_{1}}\otimes S_{k_{2}}
is not guaranteed unit trace.

III-C

Techniques, Tools and Contributions

We develop an intricate sequence of steps to handle the above mentioned challenge. Carefully respecting non-commutativity, utilizing the structure of
S_{k_{j}}
and leveraging the equivalence of purifications
[
6
, Thm. 5.1.1]
, we develop a robust technique to analyze likelihood POVMs. Refer to our analysis of terms
C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
in proof of Thm.
3
.

We highlight the power, universality and simplicity of the above developed techniques. With this work, we have now achieved the best known inner bounds for single POVM simulation in point-to-point, multiple POVM simulation in distributed and multiple POVM simulation in a distributed decoder setup
[
13
]
. Moreover, we are able to handle both one-shot (See Rem.
3
) and structured likelihood POVMs (Sec.
IV-B
) in a unified approach. This is on top of the likelihood POVMs simplicity we alluded to.

In generalizing the above approach, the distributed scenario offers

We let
\{\ket{b_{1}},\cdots,\ket{b_{d}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
denote an arbitrary but fixed orthonormal basis and
\mathcal{H}_{X}=\mathcal{H}
denote the reference space. For any
\ket{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\ket{b_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}
, we let
\ket{\olinex}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}^{*}\ket{b_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}
denote the complex conjugation with respect to orthonormal basis
\{\ket{b_{1}},\cdots,\ket{b_{d}}\}
. For an operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, we let
T^{*}
and
T^{t}
denote the unique operator for which
\innerproduct{b_{i}}{T^{*}b_{j}}=\left(\innerproduct{b_{i}}{Tb_{j}}\right)^{*}
for every
i\leq i,j\leq d
. Some properties of this conjugation map that we will have opportunity to use are proven in Lemma
6
.

III-D

Proof of Thms.
1
and
2
via Unstructured Likelihood POVMs

We provide a proof of the one-shot statement in Thm.
2
which naturally generalizes to a proof for the asymptotic regime in Thm.
1
. Let
(R,C)\in\mathscr{C}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
and specifically,
(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})\in\mathcal{T}(\rho,\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
in Defn.
2
be such that
R+C>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XWY}||\sigma_{XY}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2})
,
R>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XW}||\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2})
wrt
\sigma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sigma_{XWY}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}\otimes{\overline{\mathscr{E}}}^{{Y|W}}_{p}\circ\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{\mu})(\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho}}{\phi_{\rho}})
.

Bank of
K
Encoder POVMs
: As is standard, we design a multi-code
c
comprising of
K
codes - each a subset of elements from the outcome set
\mathcal{W}
of
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}
- each of which is used to determine the
K
POVMs of the simulation protocol. For the MCP in the PTP scenario, we do
not
require the individual codes to be partitioned into bins. However, in anticipation of distributed MCP involving bins and moreover structured likelihood POVMs, we shall construct a more general multi-code with each of the
K
codes partitioned into bins. Specifically, let
c=(c_{k}:k\in[K])
denote a multi-code comprising of
K=2^{C}
codes with the
k
-th code
c_{k}=(w(k,m,b)\in\mathcal{W}:(m,b)\in[M]\times[B])
comprise of
M=2^{R}
bins each with
B=2^{\beta}
codewords each. Associated with codeword
w(k,m,b)\in\mathcal{W}
, let
\mu_{k,m,b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mu_{w(k,m,b)}
. Associated with code
c_{k}
, we design POVM
\theta_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\theta_{k,m}:m\in[\overline{M}]\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
where

\displaystyle\theta_{k,m}=\sum_{b=1}^{B}\theta_{k,m,b},~{\theta}_{k,m,b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\upsilon({k,m,b})\frac{{S}_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\rho}\mu_{k,m,b}\sqrt{\rho}{S}_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{MB\tr(\rho\mu_{k,m,b})}\mbox{ for }m\in[M],

(8)

\displaystyle{S}_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\theta_{k,m}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\theta_{k,m,b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\!\frac{\upsilon({k,m,b})}{MB}\frac{\sqrt{\rho}\mu_{k,m,b}\sqrt{\rho}}{\tr(\rho\mu_{k,m,b})}\mbox{, and }\theta_{k,0}=I_{\mathcal{H}}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}\theta_{k,m}=I_{\mathcal{H}}-z_{k}

(9)

where
S_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, as is standard, is the generalized inverse of
S_{k}^{\frac{1}{2}}
that is
0
on the null space of
S_{k}
and

is the projector on the support of
S_{k}
.
8
8

8

In the definition of
{\theta}_{k,m,b}
,
\frac{\boldsymbol{0}}{0}
is defined as
\boldsymbol{0}
, where
\boldsymbol{0}
denotes the zero operator.
Before verifying
\theta_{k}
is indeed a POVM, we record a fact that will be useful at a later point. From the definition of
S_{k}
, we note that
\sum_{m=1}^{M}\theta_{k,m}
is the projector on the range space of
S_{k}
. We therefore have

\displaystyle\sqrt{S_{k}}\theta_{k,0}\sqrt{S_{k}}=0,~\sqrt{S_{k}}\theta_{k,m,b}\sqrt{S_{k}}=\frac{z_{k}\sqrt{\omega}\mu_{k,m,b}\sqrt{\omega}z_{k}}{\tr(\omega\mu_{k,m,b})}\mbox{ for every }(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B],

(10)

where
z_{k}
is the projector on the range space of
S_{k}
.

Owing to
\mu_{k,m,b}\geq 0
, we have
\theta_{k,m,b}\geq 0
. Since
\sum_{m=1}^{M}\theta_{k,m}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\theta_{k,m,b}=z_{k}
is a projector, the latter is dominated by
I_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
, implying
\theta_{k}=\{\theta_{k,m}:m\in[M]\}\cup\{\theta_{k,0}\}
is a POVM for
k\in[K]
. Specification of our likelihood POVMs in (
8
) and (
9
) are analogous to our description in Sec.
III-A
expect for the factor
\upsilon(k,m,b)
. This is a change-of-measure factor included to take into account the binning. We justify its presence in Remark
2
.

Remark 1
.

In comparison to the rather simple encoder POVM
\theta_{k}
above that form the POVM simulation protocol, the involved nature of of the simulation POVM in earlier studies
[
5
,
2
,
22
,
23
,
12
,
14
]
is evident. All of these essentially works borrow the encoder POVM design of
[
5
]
which is elaborated in
[
2
]
. It maybe noted that
[
2
]
defines the simulation POVM through a 3 page description starting in
[
2
, Eqn.
22
on Pg. 16]
through
[
2
, Eqn.
32
on Pg. 19]
. Moreover, the proof that operators defined therein forms a valid POVM requires the OCB of Ahlswede and Winter
[
28
]
, a tool that we will not be leveraging throughout this article. The latter requires mutually independent collection of the random operators thus restricting the generalizability of this proof. While
[
22
,
23
,
12
,
14
]
borrow this same involved POVM construction,
[
14
]
is able to prove its validity without the need for OCB. Our work goes several steps ahead since we provide a new construction.

Remark 2
.

If our random codewords
(W(k,m,b):(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B])
are picked with distribution
p_{W}(w)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\tr(\rho\mu_{w}):w\in\mathcal{W}
, then neither do we need to bin our codes nor do we need a change-of-measure factor. In other words, we could set
B=1,\beta=0
and
\upsilon(k,m,b)=1
for all
(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B]
. Throughout this proof of Thm.
2
, this is indeed going to be our choice and hence the reader can assume
B=1,\beta=0
and
\upsilon(k,m,b)=1
. We have incorporated this binning (
B>0
) and additional change-of-measure (
\upsilon(k,m,b)\neq 1
) to lend it amenable for a future generalization in Thm.
6
,
5
. We explain this generalization here to aid an informed reader connect the dots.

Suppose we consider the asymptotic setting and
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
were a finite field of size
q
. Furthermore, suppose we demand that the asymptotic block-length multi-code form a coset code, i.e. we require each code of the multi-code to be a coset of a subspace in
\mathcal{W}^{n}
of dimension
nC=\log_{q}K
. Then, to guarantee that the POVM corresponding to such a structured multi-code is optimal in simulating
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\otimes n}
, we enlarge each code of our multi-code to possess bins. The reason is when we pick a random coset code to be our code of a multi-code, individual codewords will be uniformly distributed over the entire
\mathcal{W}^{n}
(with PMF
\frac{1}{q^{n}}
). Each codeword will not be typical wrt
p_{W}
. If each bin is of size at least
2^{n(\log q-H(p_{W}))}
, then atleast one codeword in each bin will be typical wrt
p_{W}
. The above rate is just the divergence between
p_{W}
and the uniform distribution. When we analyze such a code, we will end up in a measure transformed covering (See Lemma
9
). In this case
\upsilon(k,m,b)=\frac{p_{W}(w^{n}(k.m.b))}{(1/q^{n})}
is the ratio of the two measures - the uniform and
p_{W}
. Therefore, inserting this change of measure factor and the binning index, though not employed in any one-shot proof including that of Thm.
2
, will enable us naturally prove Thm.
6
,
5
.

Bank of
K
Decoder POVMs
: The decoder is based on the standard classical one-shot hypothesis testing based channel decoder that discriminates between PMFs
p_{W}(w)=\tr(\rho\mu_{w}):w\in\mathcal{W}
and
q_{W}(w):w\in\mathcal{W}
. Here, declaring a sample as distributed according to
q_{W}
when it is actually distributed as
p_{W}
is the missed detection event. Given
m\in[M]
, let
\delta_{k}(b|m)
be the probability that this one-shot hypothesis testing based channel decoder declares
w(k,m,b)
as the legitimate codeword. We define the bank
\Delta=\left\{\Delta_{k}:k\in[K]\right\}
comprising of
K
decoder POVMs via
\Delta_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\Delta_{k,y}:y\in\mathcal{Y}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
where

\displaystyle\Delta_{k,y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\delta_{k}(b|m)p_{Y|W}(y|w(k,m,b))\outerproduct{m}{m}.

The proxy state and the key steps outlining the proof
: The central challenge in the use of the likelihood POVM is its analysis for the problem at hand. Specifically, the post measurement state
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}(\varphi_{\rho})
defined in (
2
) takes on an involved form, precluding an easy study of
\norm{\left\{ \CalO-\CalS_{\Delta,\theta}\right\}(\varphi_{\rho})}_{1}
. Instead of analyzing the outcome of
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}
on
\varphi_{\rho}
, we identify a proxy mixture state
\sigma_{k}
such that the outcome
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})
of the likelihood POVMs on this proxy mixture state takes on a simple form. We then take recourse to the triangular inequality and recognize that

(11)

(12)

where in (
12
), we have used the fact that CPTP maps shrinks the trace distance
[
6
, Eq. 9.69]
and gone one step further and introduced a proxy simulation protocol
\mathcal{S}_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}:k\in[K]
. The three terms in (
12
) portray the three tasks involved in the MCP and our proof will revolve around bounding each of these terms. The term
\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}} -\varphi_{\rho}}_{1}
portrays quantum covering and our analysis of this term, hinges on a careful choice of the proxy mixture state
\sigma_{k}
. Next,
\norm{\CalS_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}) - \CalS_{{\Delta}_{k},{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}_{1}
will represent the binning event. Specifically, we define our bank
\{(\tilde{\theta}_{k},\tilde{\Delta}_{k}):k\in[K]\}
of encoder decoder pairs as those that covey the bin index
b\in[B]
too. Bounding
\norm{\CalS_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}) - \CalS_{{\Delta}_{k},{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}_{1}
on the above will therefore yield an upper bound on the binning rate
\beta=\log_{2}B
. Finally the first term in (
12
) portrays the QC covering. As we shall see, bounding each of the terms above crucially hinges on the choice of the proxy mixed states
\sigma_{k}:k\in[K]
. A careful choice can enable to deal each of the terms elegantly. Identifying such proxy mixed states
\sigma_{k}:k\in[K]
and the necessary tools is the crux of this article.

Remark 3
.

Our solution to all the MCPs addressed in this article follows an identical sequence of steps portrayed through the breakup of terms in (
11
), (
12
) involving a QC covering, a binning term and a quantum-only covering. All of the proofs, as is this one, will be facilitated via an intelligent choice of the proxy mixed states
\sigma_{k}:k\in[K]
and the associated tools to upper bound each of the terms in a simple and elegant fashion.

Choice of the Proxy states
\sigma_{k}
and Leveraging Canonical Purifications
: We choose
\sigma_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}S_{k}
for
k\in[K]
, where
S_{k}
is as defined in (
9
). Suppose for a moment
B=1
, i.e.,
\beta=0
,
\upsilon(k,m,b)=1
and the random codebook
C=(W(k,m):(k,m)\in[K]\times[M])
were chosen mutually independently wrt distribution
(p_{W}(w)=\tr(\rho,\mu_{w}):w\in\mathcal{W})
, then it will be evident to an informed reader that
\sigma_{k}=S_{k}
can be made arbitrarily close to
\rho
by choosing
\log M+\log B>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XW}||\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2})
. However, we are required to upper bound the distance
\norm{\varphi_{S_{k}} -\varphi_{\rho}}_{1}
between their purifications. Since
\norm{S_{k}-\rho}_{1}{\leq}\norm{\varphi_{S_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}
in general, deriving an upper bound on the LHS of this inequality will not enable us derive an upper bound on the term
\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}=\norm{\varphi_{S_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}
of interest. To overcome this, we leverage the particular choice of canonical purifications. The choice of the canonical purification
[
5
]
,
[
6
, Pg. 166]
enables us to suitably reverse the above inequality. A remark on the techniques we present now is in order.

Remark 4
.

Broadly, we adopt the technique developed by Winter
[
5
, App. A, Lem. 14]
in proving an upper bound on the distance between canonical purification of close ‘states’. However, these techniques
[
5
, App. A, Lem. 14]
work only when both purifications have unit trace. Here, and more so in the distributed case (Proof of Thm.
3
), one of our purifications
\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}=\outerproduct{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}
is not necessarily unit trace. The following enhancements handle this.

Recalling
\sigma_{k}=S_{k}
we focus on one term
\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}=\norm{\varphi_{S_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}
. From Prop.
1
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}\leq\sqrt{a^{2}+b+c}\mbox{ where }a=1-\innerproduct{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}},b=4\innerproduct{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}-4\mbox{ and }c=4-4\innerproduct{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}{\varphi_{\rho}}\innerproduct{\varphi_{\rho}}{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}.

(13)

Since
\innerproduct{\varphi_{\rho}}{\varphi_{\rho}}=1
, we have
b+c=4\innerproduct{\phi_{P}}{\phi_{P}}\innerproduct{\phi_{\rho_{K}}}{\phi_{\rho_{K}}}-4\innerproduct{\phi_{P}}{\phi_{\rho_{K}}}\innerproduct{\phi_{\rho_{K}}}{\phi_{P}}\geq 0
from the Cauchy-Bunyakowski-Schwartz inequality. This enables us assert

\displaystyle\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}\leq\sqrt{a^{2}+b+c}\leq a+\sqrt{b+c}\mbox{ where }a,b\mbox{ and }c\mbox{ are as defined in }\eqref{Eqn:PTPMCPCanoPuresClseStep1}.

(14)

We begin by analyzing
c
. This will involve leveraging non-trivial bounds from
[
36
]
as done in
[
5
, App. A, Lem. 14]
. Specifically, choosing
A=\rho
,
B=\sigma_{k}
in Prop.
7
and collating bounds (
13
) to (
14
), we conclude

\displaystyle\norm{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}-\varphi_{\rho} }_{1}\leq a+\sqrt{b+c}\leq a+\sqrt{b+8\sqrt{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}}}=a+\sqrt{b+c}\leq a+\sqrt{b+8\sqrt{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}}}.

(15)

The RHS of (
15
) leaves the last term in (
12

[
15
]
or the asymptotic version (Lemma

). We shall therefore come back to this term at the end after having dealt with the first two terms, which we shall do now.

We define
\mathcal{S}_{\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\theta}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{S}_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}

The Binning Term
: We begin by identifying the two components that make up the binning term
\norm{\CalS_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}) - \CalS_{{\Delta}_{k},{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}_{1}
. From (
17
) and following the same steps as in Sec.
A-A
, it is straightforward to verify that the binning term is

\displaystyle\norm{\left\{\CalS_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}} - \CalS_{{\Delta}_{k},{\theta}_{k}}\right\}\!(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}_{1}\!\!=\norm{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{y \in\CalY}\frac{\upsilon(k,m,b)}{MB}\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}{c}p_{Y|W}(y|w(k,m,b))~~~\\-~p_{Y|W}(y|w(k,m,b^{*}_{k,m}))\end{array} \!\!\!\right)\frac{\sqrt{\rho}\mu_{k,m,b}\sqrt{\rho}}{\tr(\rho\mu_{k,m,b})}\otimes\ketbra{y}}_{1}.

Analyzing this is straight forward in both the one-shot and asymptotic case and does not rely on the use of the likelihood POVMs anymore. We therefore conclude that
\norm{\CalS_{\tilde{\Delta}_{k},\tilde{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}) - \CalS_{{\Delta}_{k},{\theta}_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}_{1}\leq\frac{\eta}{3}
if
\log B=\beta<\mathcal{D}_{H}^{\frac{\eta}{3}}(p_{W}||q_{W})+\log(9\eta^{-2})
, where
q_{W}
is a PMF on
\mathcal{W}
according to which each of the codewords
(w(k,m,b):(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B])
are picked. In the case of proof of Thm.
2
,
q_{W}=p_{W}
, letting
\beta=0
.

Collating Bounds And Identifying Bounds
: Recalling (
12
) and specifically substituting (i) (
13
), (
15
) for the third term in (
12
), (ii) (
17
), (
18
) for the first term in (
12
) and (iii) (

) for the third term in (
12
), we have

\displaystyle\norm{ \CalO(\varphi_{\rho}) -\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\CalS_{\Delta_{k},\theta_{k}}(\varphi_{\rho})}{K} }_{1}\!\!\leq\norm{ \CalO(\varphi_{\rho}) -\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\CalS_{\tldDelta_{k},\tldtheta_{k}}(\varphi_{\sigma_{k}})}{K}}_{1}\!\!+\!1\!-\tr(\sigma_{k})+2\sqrt{\tr(\sigma_{k})-1+2\sqrt{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}}}

\displaystyle+\norm{\frac{1}{MB}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{y \in\CalY}\upsilon(k,m,b)\left(p_{Y|W}(y|w(k,m,b))-p_{Y|W}(y|w(k,m,b^{*}_{k,m})) \right)\frac{\sqrt{\rho}\mu_{k,m,b}\sqrt{\rho}}{\tr(\rho\mu_{k,m,b})}\otimes\ketbra{y}}_{1},

(22)

where we have identified
\innerproduct{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}{\varphi_{\sigma_{k}}}
with
\tr(\sigma_{k})
. We now average over the ensemble of codes. Suppose
C=\left(W(k,m,b):(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B]\right)
are pairwise independent and
P(W(k,m,b)=w)=q_{W}(w)
, then we the change-of-measure factor
\upsilon(k,m,b)=\frac{p_{W}(w(k,m,b))}{q_{W}(w(k,m,b))}=\frac{\tr(\rho\mu_{w})}{q_{W}(w(k,m,b))}
and evaluate the average of (
22
) over the ensemble of codebooks with the aforementioned distribution of the codes. When we operate the
\mathbb{E}\left\{\cdot\right\}
operator on the RHS of (
22
) and employ Jensen’s inequality applied on the concave square root
\sqrt{}-
-function, we recognize

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left\{\!1\!-\tr(\sigma_{k})+2\sqrt{\tr(\sigma_{k})-1+2\sqrt{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}}}\right\}\leq 1-\mathbb{E}\left\{\tr(\sigma_{k})\right\}+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left\{\tr(\sigma_{k})\right\}-1+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left\{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}\right\}}}

(23)

With the above choice of change of measure and the evaluation of
\mathbb{E}\{\}
with respect to the code distribution
q_{W}
, it is straightforward to see from
\sigma_{k}=S_{k}
and the latter’s definition in (
9
) that
\mathbb{E}\left\{\tr(\sigma_{k})\right\}=1
. We therefore have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left\{\!1\!-\tr(\sigma_{k})+2\sqrt{\tr(\sigma_{k})-1+2\sqrt{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}}}\right\}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\sqrt[4]{\mathbb{E}\left\{\norm{\rho-\sigma_{k}}_{1}\right\}}.

(24)

With the first term on the RHS of (
22
) being a standard covering term that can be handled via either convex split or QCL (Lemma
9
) in the asymptotic setting, the LHS of (
23
) reducing to the RHS of (
24
) - another application of either convex split or QCL (Lemma
9
) in the asymptotic setting and the last term in (
22
) being a standard binning term, we end up with the following bounds
R+C>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XWY}||\sigma_{XY}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2}),R>\mathcal{D}_{\max}^{\frac{\eta}{2}}(\sigma_{XW}||\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{W})+\log(4\eta^{-2})
. This completes the proof.

IV
Distributed MCP

In this section, we consider the problem of simulating the action of a POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}})
, where
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
, on a state
\rho^{\underline{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho^{A_{1}A_{2}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}})
with its two components at distributed locations. See Fig.
2
. As we stated in Sec.
I
, we develop two kinds of likelihood POVM based simulation protocols. In Sec.
IV-A
, we present the unstructured likelihood POVM based simulation protocol which enables us derive a new inner bound
\mathscr{A}^{\mbox{\tiny U}}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda)
for the one-shot regime in Thm.
3
. Firstly, this strictly improves the current best known one-shot inner bound
[
12
, Thm. 3]
. Secondly,
\mathscr{A}^{\mbox{\tiny U}}_{\eta}(\rho,\lambda)
naturally generalizes to the current known best inner bound for the distributed MCP using unstructured likelihood POVMs. To be specific, the proof of Thm.
3
holds verbatim as a proof of Thm.
4
. Next, in Sec.
IV-B
we develop the structured likelihood POVM based simulation protocol and in Thm.
5
, we prove achievability of the current best known inner bound in the asymptotic regime. It must be noted that due to the lack of one-shot structured codes, we are unable to derive corresponding inner bounds in the one-shot regime. This is a fertile area for investigation. Before we proceed to precisely formulate the distributed MCP, we comment on the proofs of Thms.
3
,
4
and
5
we provide.

Remark 5
.

The proof of Thm.
3
serves as a unified proof of all Thms.
3
,
4
and
5
. Essentially, we upper bound the trace distance
\norm{\CalO(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})-\CalS_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}}) }_{1}
of interest (See Defn.
4
) and (
26
) below) via a sum of four terms
A,B_{\underline{k}},C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
(See (
33
)) defined in (
34
) - (
IV-A
) in all three proofs. Given this unified breakup of
\norm{\CalO(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})-\CalS_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}}) }_{1}
, the three proofs maybe viewed as involving two stages. In the first stage which forms the bulk of the proof, each term
A,B_{\underline{k}},C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
is characterized via a sequence of steps. In the second stage, appropriate covering bounds are employed for
A,C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
and a packing bound is employed for
B_{\underline{k}}
. The characterization of each term
A,B_{\underline{k}},C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
in the proof of all three Thms.
3
,
4
and
5
is identical and so is the first stage of each proof. This first stage, common to all proofs, is provided in proof of Thm.
3
only. A change-of-measure factor (
\upsilon(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})
) which is non-trivial only in proof of Thm.
5
is therefore also incorporated in proof of Thm.
3
.

The only difference across the three proofs is in the second stage. For proof of Thms.
3
and
4
, we leverage the convex split lemma
[
19
,
30
]
as our packing bound. For both these proofs, just as in proof of Thm.
2
in Sec.
III-D
, the change-of-measure factors
\upsilon(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})=1
are trivial though we employ binning in proving all three theorems. We therefore employ the tripartite convex split lemma
[
30
, Appendix A]
to upper bound
A
and the (single party) convex split lemma
[
29
]
to upper bound
C_{\underline{k}},D_{\underline{k}}
. Since proof of Thm.
5
involves a non-trivial change of measure term and is in the asymptotic regime, we resort to our asymptotic QCL (Lemma
9
) in Appendix
C
that incorporates a change of measure term. Finally, the binning term
B_{\underline{k}}
is handled in each of the theorem proofs via the standard one-shot hypothesis testing based bound. Proof of Thm.
5
differs in three aspects as elaborated in Rem.
6
.

In precisely stating the distributed MCP, we begin by enhancing our notation. When
j\in[2]
,
\text{\sout{$j$}}\in[2]
denotes complement index, i.e.
\{j,\text{\sout{$j$}}\}=\{1,2\}
. An
underline
denotes an appropriate aggregation of related objects. For ex. if
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}},\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
are Hilbert spaces,
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}
denotes
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
, whereas when
k_{j}\in[K_{j}]
for
j\in[2]
are elements in index sets,
\underline{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in[\underline{K}]
where
[\underline{K}]=[K_{1}]\times[K_{2}]
. We let
\mathcal{H}_{12}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{j}=\mathcal{H}_{A_{j}}

\rho^{j}=\rho^{A_{j}}=\tr_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}(\rho^{\underline{A}})
for
j=1,2
and
\rho^{12}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho^{A_{1}A_{2}}
.
\ket{\varphi_{\rho_{j}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{j}},\ket{\varphi_{\rho_{12}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}
denote canonical purifications of
\rho_{j},\rho_{12}
respectively. We define below a separable POVM as one whose action on a bipartite state can be simulated by separate measurements on its two components followed by stochastic processing.

Definition 3
.

A POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\lambda_{y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
acting on bipartite states is separable if there exists POVMs
\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{j}}=\{\mu_{w_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{{j}}):w_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{j}\}
for
j\in[2]
and a stochastic matrix
p_{Y|\underline{W}}=p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{w_{1},w_{2}}p_{Y|\underline{W}}(y|w_{1},w_{2})\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}=\lambda_{y}\mbox{ for all }y\in\mathcal{Y}.

(25)

We now describe the scenario of interest (Fig.
2
). Tx
j
holds system
j
of the bipartite state
\rho^{12}=\rho^{A_{1}A_{2}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}})
where
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
. The goal is to simulate the action of the separable POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\lambda_{y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{A}}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
with the classical outcome available at the Rx. To achieve this, Tx
j
shares (i)
C_{j}
bits/msmt of common (independent) randomness and (ii) a noiseless bit pipe of
R_{j}
bits/msmt, both with Rx. These two resources are used (in the one-shot setting) as follows. For
j\in[2]
, a bank of
K_{j}=2^{C_{j}}
POVMs
\theta_{k_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\theta_{k_{j},m_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j}):m_{j}\in[M_{j}]\}:k_{j}\in[K_{j}]
are designed, each with atmost
M_{j}=2^{R_{j}}
outcomes. On observing the shared random bits
k_{j}\in[K_{j}]
, Tx
j
measures component
j
of the state with the POVM
\theta_{k_{j}}
. It communicates the
R_{j}
bits of the observed outcome
m_{j}\in[M_{j}]
(Fig.
2
) to the Rx via the noiseless bit pipe. The Rx having all common random bits
\underline{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
and the observed outcomes of the
\underline{k}
-indexed POVMs, employs a corresponding bank
\Delta_{\underline{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\Delta_{\underline{k},y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{M}}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}:\underline{k}\in[\underline{K}]
of decoder POVMs to simulate the classical outcome corresponding to
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Here
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{M}}=\mathcal{H}_{M_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M_{2}}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{M_{j}}=\mbox{span}\{\ket{m_{j}}:m_{j}\in[M_{j}]\}
with-
\innerproduct{\hatm_{j}}{m_{j}}=\delta_{m_{j}\hat{m}_{j}}
. Assuming the shared random bits
\underline{k}\in[\underline{K}]
are uniformly distributed, we identify the CPTP maps corresponding to the original POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
and simulation protocol as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{X}\!\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}\right\}\!,~\mathcal{S}_{\Delta,\theta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{\underline{k}}\frac{\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{\underline{k}},\theta_{\underline{k}}}}{K_{1}K_{2}},\mbox{ where }\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{\underline{k}},\theta_{\underline{k}}}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{i_{X}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\Delta_{\underline{k}}}\!\circ\left[\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{1}}}\!\circ\!\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{2}}}\right]\!\right\}=\!\left\{\!i_{X}\!\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\Delta_{\underline{k}}}\!\circ\!\left[\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{1}}}\!\!\otimes\!\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{2}}}\!\right]\right\}.

(26)

\mathcal{H}_{X}
represents the inaccessible reference space. We enforce the outcomes of
\mathcal{O}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})
and
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})
be within a chosen
\eta
. We are thus led to the following. We should note that in replacing
\otimes
with
\circ
in the square parenthesis
\left[\cdot\right]
above, we are recognizing that
\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{1}}}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\theta_{k_{2}}}
commute. Obviously, we have to append these terms with identities in the rest of the spaces for these identities to hold.

Definition 4
.

A
(\underline{K},\underline{M},\underline{\theta},\Delta)
one-shot (POVM simulation) protocol consists of (i) bank of
K_{j}
POVMs
\theta_{k_{j}}=\{\theta_{k_{j},m_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{X_{j}}):m_{j}\in M_{j}\}
for
k_{j}\in\mathcal{K}_{j}
each with at most
M_{j}
outcomes for
j\in[2]
, and a corresponding bank of (decoder) POVMs
\Delta_{\underline{k}}=\{\Delta_{\underline{k},y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{M}}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
for
\underline{k}\in[\underline{K}]
, wherein
\mathcal{Y}
is the outcome set of the POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
to be simulated.
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on
\rho^{12}
can be
\eta
-simulated with (communication) cost
(\underline{R},\underline{C})
if there exists a
(\underline{K},\underline{M},\theta,\Delta)
one-shot POVM simulation protocol such that
\log K_{j}\leq C_{j}
,
\log M_{j}\leq R_{j}
for
j\in[2]
and

\norm{\CalO(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})-\CalS_{\Delta,\theta}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}}) }_{1}\leq\eta
where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot),\mathcal{S}_{\Delta,\theta}(\cdot)
are as defined in (
26
).
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on
\rho^{12}
can be perfectly simulated with (communication) cost
(\underline{R},\underline{C})
if for every
\eta>0
, there exists
N_{\eta}
such that for all
n\geq N_{\eta}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
’s action on
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{12}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho^{12}
can be
\eta
-simulated with cost
(n\underline{R},n\underline{C})
. We define

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\eta}(\rho^{12},\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}(\underline{R},\underline{C}):\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\mbox{'s action on }\rho^{12}\mbox{ can be }\eta\mbox{-simulated with cost }(\underline{R},\underline{C})\\
\end{array}\!\!\!\right\},\mbox{ and}\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(\rho^{12},\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}(\underline{R},\underline{C}):\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\mbox{'s action on }\rho^{12}\mbox{ can be }\mbox{perfectly simulated with cost }(\underline{R},\underline{C})\end{array}\!\!\right\}.

The object of our interest is to characterize inner bounds on
\mathscr{C}_{\eta}(\rho^{12},\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
and
\mathscr{C}(\rho^{12},\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
.

IV-A

Distributed MCP via Unstructured POVMs

We present a new inner bound to the distributed MCP in the one-shot regime.

Definition 5
.

For POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\lambda_{y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{12}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
, we let
\tau(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
denote the collection of all triples
(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
, where
(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
represents (i) POVMs
\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\mu_{w_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{{j}}):w_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{j}\}
with outcome set
\mathcal{W}_{j}
for
j\in[2]
and (ii) stochastic matrix
p_{Y|\underline{W}}=p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}
satisfying (
25
).
For a triple
(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)\in\tau(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
, we let

\displaystyle\sigma_{\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p}^{X\underline{W}Y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sigma_{\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p}^{XW_{1}W_{2}Y}\!\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}\!\otimes\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{Y|{\underline{W}}}_{p}\!\otimes\left[\mathscr{E}^{\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}}\right])\{\varphi_{\rho^{12}}\}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{w_{1},w_{2},y}\!\!\!p_{Y|\underline{W}}(y|\underline{w})\sqrt{\rho_{12}}\left(\mu_{w_{1}}\!\otimes\!\mu_{w_{2}}\right)\sqrt{\rho_{12}}\otimes\outerproduct{\ulinew~y}{\ulinew~y}

and associate the set
\mathscr{A}_{\eta}(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
of all quadruples
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq}^{4}
satisfying

\displaystyle R_{j}>\mathcal{I}(W_{j};X)-\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2}),~R_{1}+R_{2}>\mathcal{I}(\underline{W};X),~R_{j}+C_{j}>\mathcal{I}(W_{j};X,Y)-\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2}),

\displaystyle R_{1}+C_{1}+R_{2}+C_{2}>\mathcal{I}(\underline{W};XY),~R_{1}+R_{2}+C_{j}>\mathcal{I}(W_{j};X,Y)+\mathcal{I}(W_{\text{\sout{$j$}}};X)-\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2}),

R_{1}+R_{2}

\mathcal{I}(W_{1}W_{2};X)-\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2})
. The correct bound is as stated above
\mathcal{I}(W_{1}W_{2};X)=\mathcal{I}(W_{1};X_{1})+\mathcal{I}(W_{2};X_{2})-\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2})
, the RHS is what we have stated in Defn.

.

j=1,2
, where
\underline{W}=W_{1},W_{2}
and

\mathcal{I}(W_{1};W_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{\eta}{8}}_{\mbox{\tiny H}}(P_{W_{1}W_{2}}\|P_{W_{1}}\times P_{W_{2}})+\log\left(\frac{\eta}{8}\right)
,
\mathcal{I}(W_{j};X)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{D}^{\frac{\eta}{16}}_{\max}(\sigma^{W_{j}X}\|\sigma^{X}\otimes\sigma^{W_{j}})+\log\left(\frac{64}{\eta^{2}}\right)

\sigma_{\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p}^{XA_{1}A_{2}W_{1}W_{2}Y}
