]
for these definitions.

Theorem 3
.

The action of
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho^{12}
can be
\eta
-simulated in one-shot with communication cost
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})
if there exists a
(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)\in\tau(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
for which
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
.

Proof.

Let
(\underline{R},\underline{C})\in\mathscr{A}_{\eta}(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
for some
(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)\in\tau(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
. Our proof of achievability of
(\underline{R},\underline{C})
closely resembles that provided for Thm.
1
with likelihood POVMs. After stating the simulation protocol, we break the term
\norm{\left\{\CalO- \CalS_{\Delta,\theta} \right\}(\varphi_{\rho^{12}})}_{1}
of interest into three terms - QC covering, binning and quantum-only covering. The main difference will be in handling the quantum-only term - the last term. We begin defining the simulation protocol.

POVM Simulation Protocol
: Throughout,
K_{j}=2^{C_{j}},M_{j}=2^{R_{j}},B_{j}=2^{\beta_{j}}\in\mathbb{N}
and
[K_{j}],[M_{j}],[B_{j}]
denote common randomness, message and bin index sets respectively. Since, the two POVM outcomes are correlated, a Slepian-Wolf
[
38
]
binning can reduce message rates
R_{1},R_{2}
. The outcomes are therefore binned and the bin index
b_{j}\in[B_{j}]
is not communicated to the Rx. Specifically, for
j\in[2]
, let
c_{j}=(c_{k_{j}}:k_{j}\in[K_{j}])
denote a multi-code comprising of
K_{j}=2^{C_{j}}
codes wherein the
k_{j}-
th code is
c_{j}=(w_{j}(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})\in\mathcal{W}_{k_{j}}:(m_{j},b_{j})\in[M_{j}]\times[B_{j}])
comprises of
M_{j}=2^{R_{j}}
bins each with
B_{j}=2^{\beta_{j}}
codewords. Associated with codeword,
w_{j}(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})
, let
\mu_{k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mu_{w_{j}(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
. Associated with code
c_{k_{j}}
, we define POVM
\theta_{k_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\theta_{k_{j},m_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j}):m_{j}\in[\overline{M}_{j}]\right\}
, wherein

\displaystyle\theta_{k_{j},m_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{b_{j}=1}^{B_{j}}\theta_{k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}},\theta_{k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\upsilon(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})S_{k_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\rho^{j}}\mu_{k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}}\sqrt{\rho^{j}}S_{k_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{M_{j}B_{j}p_{W_{j}}(w(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}))}\mbox{ for }m\in[M],~S_{k_{j}}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\sum_{m_{j}=1}^{M_{j}}\theta_{k_{j},m_{j}},\mbox{ i.e.}

(30)

\displaystyle R_{2}+\beta_{2}>I(W_{2};X)_{\sigma_{2}},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!R_{2}+C_{2}+\beta_{2}>I(W_{2};XY)_{\sigma},

(83)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}<I(W_{1};W_{2}),~\beta_{j}>0:j\in[2],

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!R_{1}+R_{2}+C_{1}+C_{2}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}>I(\underline{W};XY)_{\sigma}+I(W_{1};W_{2})_{\sigma}.

(84)

for
j\in[2]
, where all information quantities are evaluated wrt state
\sigma_{\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p}^{XA_{1}A_{2}W_{1}W_{2}Y}=\sigma_{\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p}^{X{1}{2}W_{1}W_{2}Y}
.
12
12

12

Recall our notational simplification
\rho^{A_{1}A_{2}}=\rho^{12}
. The
A_{j}
system can also be denoted as the
j
system.
The bounds in (
72
) - (
75
) are obtained by eliminating
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
via standard Fourier-Motzkin elimination of the bounds in (
82
) - (
84
). This completes our proof.
∎

IV-B

Distributed MCP via Structured POVMs

In this section, we demonstrate how our techniques are universal in the sense that we can recover the current best known bound using structured POVMs derived in
[
14
]
. We begin by very briefly explaining the central idea behind why structured POVMs - POVMs based on structured codes - can yield strictly larger inner bounds for distributed MCP for simulating the action of certain ‘coupled POVMs’. If we refer back to the proof of Thm.
4
, it is evident that the simulation protocol attempts to and relies on communicating the actual
pair
of outcomes
(\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(k_{1},m_{1},b_{1}),\boldsymbol{w}_{2}(k_{2},m_{2},b_{2}))
to the decoder (Rx). Suppose in simulating the action of a separable POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on a bipartite state
\rho^{12}
, we do not require the decoder to recover the pair of outcomes, but only requires a specific compressive function of the outcomes. As a concrete example, suppose POVMs
\lambda_{\mathcal{W}_{1}}
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{W}_{2}}
are two POVMs with outcome sets
\mathcal{W}_{1}=\mathcal{W}_{2}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
being a common finite field of size
q
. Furthermore, suppose
p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}
is a stochastic matrix satisfying
p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}(y|w_{1},w_{2})=p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}(y|\tilde{w}_{1},\tilde{w}_{2})
whenever
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}=\tilde{w}_{1}\oplus\tilde{w}_{2}
and (
25
), where
\oplus
is the field addition within field
\mathcal{F}_{q}
. We then define a stochastic matrix
p_{Y|Z}
with input in
\mathcal{W}
, output in
\mathcal{Y}
via
p_{Y|Z}(y|w_{1}\oplus w_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}(y|w_{1},w_{2})
for
(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\times\mathcal{W}_{2}
and note that

\displaystyle\lambda_{y}=\sum_{w_{1},w_{2}}p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}(y|w_{1},w_{2})\left[\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}\right]=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{W}}p_{Y|Z}(y|z)\sum_{w_{1},w_{2}}\left[\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}\right]\mathds{1}_{\left\{z=w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\right\}}~\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

(85)

This implies that the decoder only needs to recover the letter-wise sum of the outcomes and post process the same via the stochastic map
p_{Y|Z}
to simulate the action of
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho^{12}
. Next, let us revisit how we enable the decoder in proof of Thm.
3
,
4
recover the pair
(w_{1}(k_{1},m_{1},b_{1}),w_{2}(k_{2},m_{2},b_{2}))
of outcomes via binning. Observe that the Rx is provided only
(k_{1},k_{2})
and
(m_{1},m_{2})
. The decoder identifies the pair
(b_{1},b_{2})
of bin indices via a Slepian-Wolf decoder. In the asymptotic case, it looks for a pair of codewords in the indexed bins that are jointly typical wrt
(p_{W_{1}W_{2}}(w_{1},w_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\tr(\rho^{12}[\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}]):(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\times\mathcal{W}_{2})
. This yields us the bound
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}<\Gamma=H(p_{W_{1}})+H(p_{W_{2}})-H(p_{W_{1}W_{2}})
. Any coding scheme that increases the value of the bound
\Gamma
provides us with advantages since the bin indices are not communicated and this results in a larger saving of sum rate. If instead of picking the bins of each code
c_{j}
independently, suppose we choose the bins of each multi-code of both codebooks
c_{1},c_{2}
to be cosets of a common linear code. Now, observe that owing to the coset structure of the bins, the sum
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(k_{1},m_{1},b_{1})\oplus\boldsymbol{w}_{2}(k_{2},m_{2},b_{2})
is contained within the
sum of the two bins
i.e., the collection of codewords obtained by adding every pair of codewords in the two bins. However, the coset structure implies this collection of sums is of size exactly equal to the size of the coset. This gives us a reduction in the size of the actual sum with the set of all possible sums. This yields us rate gains. We refer to
[
39
, Rem. 1, Ex. 1,2]
for more details. The upshot of this discussion is that, when contrasted with proof of Thm.
3
, there are only three changes in the proof of Thm.
5
which we summarize in Rem.
6
below.

Definition 7
.

For POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}=\{\lambda_{y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\underline{X}}):y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
, we let
\tau_{\oplus}(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
denote the collection of all quadruples
(\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q},\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{W}},\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W_{1}\oplus W_{2}})
, where (i)
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
is the finite field of size prime power
q
, (ii)
\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{W}}=\{\mu^{j}_{w}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{X_{j}}):w\in\mathcal{W}\}
is a POVM on component
j
with outcome set
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
and
p_{Y|W_{1}\oplus W_{2}}(y|w_{1}\oplus w_{2})
is a stochastic matrix such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{y}=\sum_{w_{1},w_{2}}p_{Y|W_{1}\oplus W_{2}}(y|w_{1}\oplus w_{2})\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}=\lambda_{y}~\forall y\in\mathcal{Y}.

(86)

For
(\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q},\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{W}},\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W_{1}\oplus W_{2}})\in\tau_{\oplus}(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
, let
\mathscr{A}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)
be the set of all
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{4}
such that

\displaystyle R_{j}>I(W_{j};X)+I(Z;W_{\text{\sout{$j$}}})-I(W_{1};W_{2}),~R_{j}+C_{j}>I(W_{j};XY)+I(Z;W_{\text{\sout{$j$}}})-I(W_{1};W_{2})

(87)

\displaystyle R_{1}+C_{1}+R_{2}+C_{2}>I(W_{1},W_{2};XY)+I(W_{1};Z)+I(W_{2};Z)-I(W_{1};W_{2})

(88)

where
\text{\sout{$j$}}\in[2]
denotes complement index, i.e.,
\{j,\text{\sout{$j$}}\}=\{1,2\}
,
\underline{W}=W_{1},W_{2}
,
Z=W_{1}\oplus W_{2}
and all information quantities are computed with respect to state
\sigma^{XW_{1}W_{2}ZY}=(i_{X}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{YZ|W_{1}W_{2}}\circ[\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{1}}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\mu_{2}}])\{\varphi_{\rho_{X_{1}X_{2}}}\}
, where
p_{YZ|W_{1}W_{2}}(y,z|w_{1},w_{2})=p_{Y|W_{1}W_{2}}(y|w_{1},w_{2})\mathds{1}_{z=w_{1}\oplus w_{2}}
.

Theorem 5
.

The action of
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho_{X_{1}X_{2}}
can be perfectly simulated with communication cost
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})
if there exists a quadruple denote the collection of all quadruples
(\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q},\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{W}},\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W_{1}\oplus W_{2}})\in\tau_{\oplus}(\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
for which
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathscr{A}_{\oplus}(\underline{\mathcal{W}},\mu,p)
.

Proof.

We begin our proof with a remark that identifies the new elements in comparison with proof of Thm.
3
.

Remark 6
.

The simulation protocol and the proof of Thm.
5
differs in three aspects. First, is the random codebooks
C_{j}:j=1,2
. The bins of both codebooks
C_{1},C_{2}
are chosen to be cosets of a common linear code on
\mathcal{W}^{n}
. This entails a nested coset code. A random generator matrix
G_{I}\in\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\beta\times n}
is chosen by picking each entry IID and uniform. This serves as the generator matrix of a linear code whose cosets form the bins of both codes
C_{1},C_{2}
. Observe that this brings in a relationship between
C_{1},C_{2}
and furthermore restricts the codewords of a bin to possess algebraic closure property. The first upshot of this from a proof point of view is that the marginal distribution of each code word of any bin is uniformly distributed over
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{n}
. We therefore enlarge our bin and incorporate a non-trivial change-of-measure term
\upsilon(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})=\frac{p_{W_{j}}^{n}(w_{j}^{n}(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j}))}{1/q^{n}}
. This results in a bump in our covering rates. However, this bump is obtained back as a rebate in an increased binning rate. To understand this, we have provided a proof of the single PTP MCP with structured POVMs in Sec.
D-A
of Appendix
D
. In spite of using structured POVMs, i.e., based on coset codes and inspite of suffering an aforementioned bump in the covering rate, we can achieve the optimal communication cost. This is because we can enlarge the binning rate since an exponential number of codewords are not even typical according to
p_{W}
and the decoder, provided with no information, can disambiguate amidst the sea of non-typical codewords. For details see the proof of Thm.
6
in Sec.
D-A
of Appendix
D
. This can therefore be viewed as our second change. The third change is the crucial one. The change in the decoder to decode the sum instead of decoding the pair. This change manifests in a change on the bound on the sum binning rate
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}
. As we discussed earlier, the change of the decoding, the change in the structure of the code guaranteeing the number of distinct sums does not blow up combine to give us the rate advantages.

From the viewpoint of proof, we only see three corresponding changes (1) the structure of the random codes, (2) the altered covering bounds we obtain as a consequence of a non-trivial change of measure and (3) the change in the decoder resulting in an altered bound on the binning rate. Except for these changes, there are none. Essentially the first stage of the proof of Thm.
5
is identical to the proof of Thm.
3
. The following equivalent characterization of
\mathscr{A}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)
specified via pre-Fourier Motzkin elimination bounds portrays the changes vividly.

Let us now specify an equivalent characterization of
\mathscr{A}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)
via the pre-Fourier Motzkin bounds.

Lemma 5
.

For a quadruple
(\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q},\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{1}},\mu_{\mathcal{W}_{2}},p)
where
\mathcal{F}_{q}
is a field of size
q
with field addition denoted
\oplus
, let
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)
be defined as the set of all
(R_{1},R_{2},C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{4}
for which there exists
\beta_{1}\geq 0,\beta_{2}\geq 0
satisfying

\displaystyle R_{1}+\beta_{1}>I(W_{1};X)_{\sigma_{1}}+\log q-H(p_{W_{1}}),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!R_{1}+C_{1}+\beta_{1}>I(W_{1};XY)_{\sigma}+\log q-H(p_{W_{1}})

(89)

\displaystyle R_{2}+\beta_{2}>I(W_{2};X)_{\sigma_{2}}+\log q-H(p_{W_{2}}),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!R_{2}+C_{2}+\beta_{2}>I(W_{2};XY)_{\sigma}+\log q-H(p_{W_{2}})

(90)

\displaystyle\beta_{j}<\log q-H(p_{W_{1}\oplus{W_{2}}})\mbox{ for }j=1,2,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{j=1}^{2}R_{j}+C_{j}+\beta_{j}>I(\underline{W};XY)_{\sigma}+2\log q-H(\underline{W}).

(91)

Then, we have
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)={\mathscr{A}}_{\oplus}(\mathcal{W},\mu,p)
.

Proof.

If we eliminate
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
from the inequalities (

) - (
91
) via Fourier Motzkin elimination, we end up with the bounds in (
87
), (
88
).
∎

We now revert back to proof of Thm.
5
.
From Lemma
5
, it suffices to prove the bounds in (
89
) - (
91
). Contrasting the bounds in (
89
), (
90
) with those of the analogous bounds in (
82
), (
83
), we see an additional term
\log q-H(p_{W_{j}})
. This bump in the lower bound is due to the change-of-measure factor owing to the fact that our codewords are uniformly distributed. Observe that the bump in the lower bound on
R_{1}+C_{1}+\beta_{1}+R_{2}+C_{2}+\beta_{2}
in (
84
) is also
\log q-H(p_{W_{1}})+\log q-H(p_{W_{2}})
. Indeed
I(\underline{W};XY)_{\sigma}+2\log q-H(\underline{W})=I(\underline{W};XY)_{\sigma}+I(W_{1};W_{2})+2\log q-H(W_{1})-H(W_{2})
. Hence, save for these bumps in the lower bound due to the change of measure factor, all of the lower bounds due to covering in (
89
) - (
91
) are identical to corresponding lower bounds in (
82
) - (
84
). Given how we reduced the factors
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{C}_{\underline{k}},\boldsymbol{D}_{\underline{k}}
in proof of Thms.
3
,
4
to standard covering, it therefore suffices to prove the standard PTP MCP via structured POVMs. Proof of Thm.

in Sec.

of Appendix
D
facilitates this. We are only left to prove the packing upper bound on
\beta_{j}
, i.e., the upper bound
\beta_{j}<\log q-H(p_{W_{1}\oplus W_{2}})
. This is just a classical packing argument that follows straight forward from several similar analysis undertaken in
[
26
]
and can directly be plugged and played. Lastly, we specify the code construction and POVM simulation protocol.

Simulation Protocol Based on Structured POVMs
: For
j\in[2]
, let
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho_{j}^{\otimes n}
,
g_{c_{j}}\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa_{j}\times n}
,

and
g_{b_{j}}\in\mathcal{W}^{\beta_{j}\times n}
be generator matrices,
d^{n}_{j}\in\mathcal{W}^{n}
be a bias vector,
K_{j}=\mathcal{W}^{\kappa_{j}},B_{j}=\mathcal{W}^{\beta_{j}},M_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{W}^{r_{j}}
. Crucially, if
\beta_{j}\leq\beta_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}
, then the first
\beta_{j}
rows of
g_{b_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}}
is the
g_{\beta_{j}}

\beta_{j}\leq\beta_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}

g_{b_{j}}
forms the first
\beta_{j}
rows of
g_{b_{\text{\sout{$j$}}}}
. Let
w_{j}^{n}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(w_{j}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})_{1},\cdots,w_{j}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\underline{k}_{j}g_{c_{j}}\oplus\underline{m}_{j}g_{m_{j}}\oplus\underline{b}_{j}g_{b_{j}}\oplus d^{n}_{j}
for

. Let
\upsilon_{j}:\mathcal{W}^{\kappa_{j}}\times\mathcal{W}^{r_{j}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta_{j}}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is defined as
\upsilon_{j}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})={q^{n}\tr(\mu_{\ulinek_{j},\ulinem_{j},\ulineb_{j}}\boldrho_{j})}
. Let
c_{j}=(c_{\underline{k}_{j}}:\underline{k}_{j}\in[K_{j}])
denote a multi-code comprising of

codes wherein the
\underline{k}_{j}-

bins each with

codewords. Associated with codeword,
w_{j}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})
, let
\otimes_{t=1}^{n}\mu_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mu_{w_{j}(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})_{t}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j})
. Associated with code
c_{\underline{k}_{j}}
, we define POVM
\theta_{\underline{k}_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\left\{\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{j}):\underline{m}_{j}\in[\overline{M}_{j}]\right\}
, wherein

\displaystyle\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{\underline{b}_{j}=1}^{B_{j}}\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}},\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\upsilon(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})S_{\underline{k}_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{j}}\mu_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{j}}S_{\underline{k}_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{M_{j}B_{j}p_{W_{j}}(w(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}))}\mbox{ for }m\in[M],~S_{\underline{k}_{j}}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\sum_{\underline{m}_{j}=1}^{M_{j}}\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j}},\mbox{ i.e.}

(92)

\displaystyle S_{\underline{k}_{j}}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\sum_{\underline{m}_{j}=1}^{M_{j}}\sum_{\underline{b}_{j}=1}^{B_{j}}\!\!\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}}\!\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\!\sum_{\underline{m}_{j}=1}^{M_{j}}\!\sum_{\underline{b}_{j}=1}^{B_{j}}\!\!\frac{\upsilon(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j})\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{j}}\mu_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{j}}}{M_{j}B_{j}p_{W_{j}}(w(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j},\underline{b}_{j}))},\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}I_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\!-\!\sum_{\underline{m}_{j}=1}^{M_{j}}\!\!\theta_{\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j}}=I_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\!-\!z_{\underline{k}_{j}}

(93)

where
p_{W_{j}}(w_{j})=\tr(\rho^{j}\mu_{w_{j}})
for
w_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{j}
for
j\in[2]
,
\upsilon(k_{j},m_{j},b_{j})
is the change-of-measure term. It is straightforward to verify
\theta_{j}
is a valid POVM. This can be done by mimicking the steps in establishing
\theta_{k}
in (
8
) is a POVM and we shall not repeat it here.

The decoder CPTP map is standard hypothesis-testing based one-shot decoder followed by a stochastic map. Specifically, suppose
\left(p_{\underline{W}}(w_{1},w_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\tr(\rho_{12}[\mu_{w_{1}}\otimes\mu_{w_{2}}]):(w_{1},w_{2})\in\underline{\mathcal{W}}\right)
be the joint PMF and

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)=\sum_{w_{1}\in\mathcal{W}}\sum_{w_{2}\in\mathcal{W}}p_{W_{1}W_{2}}(w_{1},w_{2})\mathds{1}_{\{z=w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\}}

Consider a hypothesis-testing based one-shot decoder that tests between
p_{Z}
and the uniform distribution
(q_{Z}(z)=\frac{1}{q}:z\in\mathcal{W})
. Given
(\underline{k}_{j},\underline{m}_{j}):j\in[2]
and both codes
c_{1},c_{2}
let
\delta_{\underline{k}}(\underline{b}|\underline{m}_{1}\oplus\underline{m}_{2})
be the probability with which the decoder chooses bin index
\underline{b}\in\mathcal{W}^{\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}}
within the bin indexed
(m_{1}\oplus m_{2})
within the pair
c_{k_{1}},c_{k_{2}}
of codes. We define our decoder
\Delta_{\underline{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\Delta_{\underline{k},y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}:y\in\mathcal{Y}\}
via

\displaystyle\Delta_{\underline{k},y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\sum_{\underline{b}}\delta_{\underline{k}}(\underline{b}|\underline{m}_{1}\oplus\underline{m}_{2})p_{Y|Z}^{n}(y^{n}|w_{1}^{n}(\underline{k}_{1},\underline{m}_{1},\underline{b}_{1})\oplus w_{2}^{n}(\underline{k}_{2},\underline{m}_{2},\underline{b}_{2}))\outerproduct{m_{1}~ m_{2}}{m_{1} ~m_{2}}.

The above is a standard hypothesis based decoder that disambiguates between the uniform distribution and
p_{Z}=p_{W_{1}\oplus W_{2}}
on
\mathcal{W}
. It is straight forward to recognize that a standard classical hypothesis testing analysis yields the bounds
\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}<\log q-H(p_{Z})=D(p_{Z}||\mbox{\small unif. dist.})
. Having provided all the non-trivial elements of the proof on top of the proof of Thm.
4
, we conclude this proof here.

∎

Appendix A
POVM Simulation with respect to Purifications

We recall notation that is adopted throughout the article. We let
\{\ket{b_{1}},\cdots,\ket{b_{d}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{A}
denote an arbitrary but fixed orthonormal basis and
\mathcal{H}_{X}=\mathcal{H}_{A}
. For any
\ket{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\ket{b_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
, we let
\ket{\olinex}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}^{*}\ket{b_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
denote the complex conjugation with respect to orthonormal basis
\{\ket{b_{1}},\cdots,\ket{b_{d}}\}
. Similarly, for
A\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{H})
with a spectral decomposition
A=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\outerproduct{a_{i}}{a_{i}}
, we let
A^{*}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}A^{t}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}\outerproduct{\olinea_{i}}{\olinea_{i}}
.

13

13

This notation can be justified by the fact that the matrices of
A
and and

in the orthonormal basis

are complex conjugates or transposes of each other.

.

1.

, and

Appendix B
Facts Used in Analysis of
T_{2}

Prop 1
.

For any
\ket{\alpha},\ket{\beta}\in\mathcal{H}
with
\innerproduct{\beta}{\beta}=1
and arbitrary
\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\ketbra{\alpha}-\ketbra{\beta}}_{1}\leq\sqrt{(1-\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha})^{2}+4\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}-4\innerproduct{\alpha}{\beta}\innerproduct{\beta}{\alpha}}\leq.

(113)

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\ket{\beta^{\perp}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\ket{a}-\innerproduct{\beta}{\alpha}\ket{\beta}}{\norm{\ket{a}-\braket{\beta}{\alpha}\ket{\beta}}_{1}},c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\innerproduct{\beta}{\alpha}}{\norm{\alpha}_{1}},s\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\innerproduct{\beta^{\perp}}{\alpha}}{\norm{\alpha}_{1}}

and denoting
N\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\norm{\alpha}_{1}^{2}
, verify that

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\beta^{\perp}}{\beta}=0,|c|^{2}+|s|^{2}=1,\ket{\alpha}=\sqrt{N}(c\ket{\beta}+s\ket{\beta^{\perp}})

(114)

\displaystyle\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}-\outerproduct{\beta}{\beta}=(N|c|^{2}-1)\outerproduct{\beta}{\beta}+N|s|^{2}\outerproduct{\beta^{\perp}}{\beta^{\perp}}

\displaystyle+Ncs^{*}\outerproduct{\beta}{\betap}+Nsc^{*}\outerproduct{\betap}{\beta}.

Our goal is to compute the sum of the absolute values of the eigenvalues of the matrix

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}N|c|^{2}-1&Ns^{*}c\\
Nsc^{*}&N|s|^{2}\end{array}\right].

Specifically, we are required to compute the sum of the absolute values of the roots of the quadratic equation

\displaystyle(N|c|^{2}-1-x)(N|s|^{2}-x)-N^{2}|s|^{2}|c|^{2}~~~~

\displaystyle~~~~=x^{2}+\left[1-N(|c|^{2}+|s|^{2})\right]x-N|s|^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~=x^{2}-\left[1-N\right]x-N|s|^{2}.

The product of the roots being negative, the sum of the absolute values is twice the discriminant of this quadratic equation which is given by
\sqrt{(1-N)^{2}+4N|s|^{2}}=\sqrt{(1-N)^{2}+4N-4N|c|^{2}}
which is given by

\displaystyle\sqrt{(1-\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha})^{2}+4\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}-4\innerproduct{\beta}{\alpha}\innerproduct{\alpha}{\beta}}

(118)

∎

Prop 2
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
(1-x^{2})\leq 2(1-x)
.

Prop 3
.

For any
x,y\geq 0
,
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
.

Proof.

Consider a right angled triangle with side lengths
\sqrt{x}
and
\sqrt{y}
. The hypotenuse has length
\sqrt{x+y}
. The sum of two sides being always larger than the third side, we have
\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}\geq\sqrt{x+y}
.
∎

Appendix C
Quantum Covering Lemma

We formulate and prove a slightly general quantum covering
[
28
]

[
6
, Chap. 17]
that permits choosing the random density operators according to a different distribution than that is used in the averaging.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite dimensional Hilbert space of dimension
d
,
\mathcal{X}
be a finite set and for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\rho_{x}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
be density operators. Let
p_{X}
and
q_{X}
be two distributions on
\mathcal{X}
and
\sigma=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{X}}q_{X}(u)\rho_{u}
. Suppose
C=(X^{n}(m)\in\mathcal{X}^{n}:1\leq m\leq M)
be a collection of
M
pairwise independent and identically distributed vectors with
\mathbf{P}(X^{n}(m)=x^{n})=\prod_{t=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{t})
for each
m\in[M]
. Then, there exists
\eta>0
such that for sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{\sigma^{\otimes n} - A }_{1}\right\}\leq\exp\left\{-n\left[\frac{\log M}{n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q_{x}(x)H(\rho_{x})-D(q_{X}||p_{X})[1+4\eta]-H(\rho)\right]\right\},

\displaystyle\mbox{ where }A\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\!\!A(m),~A(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\rho_{X^{n}(m)},~~\rho_{x^{n}}=\displaystyle\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{x_{t}}~\mbox{ for any $x^{n}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}$.}

Proof.

Before we step into the outline and details of the proof, let us identify the unconditional and conditional typical projectors. Towards that end, let
\mathcal{Y}=[d]
and for
x\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\rho_{x}=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}r_{Y|X}(y|x)\outerproduct{e_{y|x}}{e_{y|x}}
be a spectral decomposition. Similarly, let
\sigma=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q_{X}(x)\rho_{x}
admit a spectral decomposition
\sigma=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}s_{Y}(y)\outerproduct{f_{y}}{f_{y}}
. We define the conditional typical projector with respect to the joint distribution
q_{X}r_{Y|X}
and the (unconditional) typical projectors with respect to the distribution
s_{Y}
as

\displaystyle\pi_{x^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\displaystyle\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\outerproduct{e_{y_{t}|x_{t}}}{e_{y_{t}|x_{t}}}\mathds{1}_{\{(x^{n},y^{n})\in T_{\eta}(q_{X}r_{Y|X})\}},~~\pi_{m}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\pi_{X^{n}(m)},~~\pi\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\displaystyle\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\outerproduct{f_{y_{t}}}{f_{y_{t}}}\mathds{1}_{\{y^{n}\in T_{4\eta}(s_{Y})\}}.

Our technique is based on Cuff’s proof of classical soft covering
[
40
, Lemma 19]
. We recognize that
\overline{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\{A\}=\sigma^{\otimes n}
and hence the quantity of interest is
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{\overline{A} - A }_{1}\right\}
. We identify three terms
T_{1},T_{2}
and
T_{3}
given by
T_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{{A} - {B} }_{1}\right\}
,
T_{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{B - \overline{B} }_{1}\right\}
and
T_{3}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{\overline{B} - \overline{A} }_{1}\right\}
where
A
is as defined in the theorem statement,

\displaystyle B=\!\sum_{m=1}^{M}\!\!\frac{B(m)}{M},~B(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi,~\overline{B}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\{B\}\mbox{ and we recognize }\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{\overline{A} - A }_{1}\right\}\leq T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}.

Our proof involves analyzing each of
T_{1},T_{2}
and
T_{3}
. As we shall see
T_{1},T_{3}
can be bounded on the using properties of unconditional and conditional typical projectors and
T_{2}
is the important term yielding our rate constraints, i.e, the lower bound on
\frac{\log M}{n}
. We therefore begin analyzing
T_{2}
.

An Upper Bound on
T_{2}
:
For ease of reference, we recall
T_{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{B - \overline{B} }_{1}\right\}
. As in proof of
[
40
, Lemma 19]
, we leverage the concavity and monotonicity properties of the square-root function. While this is rather straight forward for real numbers in
[
40
, Lemma 19]
, we require operator monotonicity and operator concavity of the square root function. As proven in the Lowner-Heinz theorem
[
41
, Thm. 2.6]
), this is indeed true. The operator square root function is operator monotonic and operator concave. We therefore have

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{B-\overline{B}}_{1}\right\}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\tr(\sqrt{\left[B-\overline{B}\right]\left[B-\overline{B}\right]^{\dagger}})\right\}=\tr(\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ \sqrt{\left[B-\overline{B}\right]\left[B-\overline{B}\right]^{\dagger}}\right\})

(119)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tr( \sqrt{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ \left[B-\overline{B}\right]\left[B-\overline{B}\right]^{\dagger}\right\}})=\tr( \sqrt{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ BB^{\dagger}\right\} - \Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ B \right\} \Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ B^{\dagger} \right\} })

where the last equality follows from
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B\overline{B}^{\dagger}\right\}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\overline{B}{B}^{\dagger}\right\}=\overline{B}~\overline{B}^{\dagger}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B^{\dagger}\right\}
. To reduce clutter in notation, we henceforth abbreviate

,
\rho_{m}=\rho_{X^{n}(m)},\zeta_{m}=\frac{q_{m}}{p_{m}}
. With this and recognizing the Hermitian nature of B, we have

\displaystyle BB^{\dagger}\!=\!\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\zeta_{m}^{2}\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi}{M^{2}}+\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b=1\\
b\neq a\end{subarray}}^{M}\frac{\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}\pi\pi_{a}\rho_{a}\pi_{a}\pi\pi\pi_{b}\rho_{b}\pi_{b}\pi}{M^{2}},~\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\{B\}=\!\!\!\sum_{u^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}\!\!\!\!q_{X}^{n}(u^{n})\pi\pi_{u^{n}}\rho_{u^{n}}\pi_{u^{n}}\pi

(120)

where we have used the identically distributed nature of
X^{n}(m):m\in[M]
in obtaining the last equality above. The pairwise independent nature of the distribution of the collection
(X^{n}(m):1\leq m\leq M)
implies

(121)

Substituting (

) and (

) in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{BB^{\dagger}\right\}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B^{\dagger}\right\}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\!\frac{1}{M^{2}}\!\!\!\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m}^{2}\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\right\}-\frac{1}{M}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{n}\in\\
\mathcal{X}^{n}\end{subarray}}q_{X}^{n}(x^{n})\pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}\pi\right)^{2}.

(122)

Since
\displaystyle\sum_{{x^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}}q_{X}^{n}(x^{n})\pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}\pi

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{BB^{\dagger}\right\}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B\right\}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{B^{\dagger}\right\}}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\!\frac{1}{M^{2}}\!\!\!\sum_{m=1}^{M}\zeta_{m}^{2}\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\right\}}~~~~\mbox{ and hence }

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq\tr( \sqrt{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ BB^{\dagger}\right\} - \Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ B \right\} \Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ B^{\dagger} \right\} })\leq\frac{1}{M}\tr(\sqrt{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}} \left\{\zeta_{m}^{2}\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\pi\pi_{m}\rho_{m}\pi_{m}\pi\right\}}).

In the following, we focus on bounding the operator under the square root in (

) through a sequence of operator inequalities. Using the basic facts (i)
ABA^{\dagger}\leq ACA^{\dagger}
whenever
B\leq C
, (ii)
\pi^{2}=\pi\leq I
, (iii)
\pi_{x^{n}}^{2}=\pi_{x^{n}}\leq I
we have

(124)

(125)

(126)

(127)

where (i) the restricted sum in the first inequality of (

\pi_{x^{n}}

, (ii) the equality in (

\pi_{x^{n}}
and
\rho_{x^{n}}

for any

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq\exp\{-\frac{n}{2}\left(\frac{\log M}{n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q_{x}(x)H(\rho_{x})-D(q_{X}||p_{X})[1+4\eta]+H(\rho)\right)\}\tr(\sqrt{\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{n}{2}\left(\frac{\log M}{n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q_{x}(x)H(\rho_{x})-D(q_{X}||p_{X})[1+4\eta]-H(\rho)\right)\right\}

This completes our analysis of

\displaystyle A_{t}(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}A(m)\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}},~A_{nt}(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}A(m)\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}},~B_{t}(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}B(m)\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}},

\displaystyle B_{nt}(m)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}B(m)\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\mbox{ and note }A(m)=A_{t}(m)+A_{nt}(m),B(m)=B_{t}(m)+B_{nt}(m).~~~~

We now make note of a fact that we will have opportunity to use on multiple occasions. From the gentle measurement lemma
[
6
, Lem. 9.4.2]
, we have
\norm{\rho_{x^{n}} - \pi_{x^{n} } \rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}}_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi_{x^{n}}]\rho_{x^{n}})}
. Next, from the measurement on close states
[
6
, Corollary 9.1.1, Exercise 9.1.8]
and combining the above inequality, we have

\displaystyle\tr(\pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}})\geq\tr(\pi\rho_{x^{n}})-\norm{\rho_{x^{n}} - \pi_{x^{n} } \rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}}_{1}\geq\tr(\pi\rho_{x^{n}})-2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi_{x^{n}}]\rho_{x^{n}})}.

(129)

Once again, leveraging the gentle measurement lemma
[
6
, Lem. 9.4.2]
, this time on the ‘sub-normalized state’
\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}

[
6
, Exercise 9.4.1]
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}} - \pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}\pi}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi]\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}} )}=2\sqrt{\tr(\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}})-\tr(\pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}})}

(130)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\tr(\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}})-\tr(\pi\rho_{x^{n}})+2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi_{x^{n}}]\rho_{x^{n}})}},

where we utilized (
129
) in the last inequality. Combining (
130
) with the bound on
\norm{\rho_{x^{n}} - \pi_{x^{n} } \rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}}_{1}
derived prior to (

\displaystyle\norm{\rho_{x^{n}} - \pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}\pi}_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi_{x^{n}}]\rho_{x^{n}})}+4\sqrt{\tr(\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}})-\tr(\pi\rho_{x^{n}})+2\sqrt{\tr([I-\pi_{x^{n}}]\rho_{x^{n}})}}.

(131)

For
x^{n}\in T_{\eta}(q_{X})
we conclude from the properties of conditional and unconditional typical projectors
[

that

\displaystyle\norm{\rho_{x^{n}} - \pi\pi_{x^{n}}\rho_{x^{n}}\pi_{x^{n}}\pi}_{1}\leq 2^{-n\frac{\eta}{10}}

(132)

for sufficiently large
n
. We are now set to analyze

.

An Upper Bound on
T_{1}
:
Since
A=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}A(m)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}[A_{t}(m)+A_{nt}(m)]
and
B=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}B(m)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}[B_{t}(m)+B_{nt}(m)]
, we have

\displaystyle T_{1}\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{ A-B}_{1}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{A_{t}(m) - B_{t}(m)}_{1}\right\}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\norm{A_{nt}(m) - B_{nt}(m)}_{1}\right\}

(133)

\displaystyle\!\!\!=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\norm{\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} }_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle~~~~~~+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\norm{\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}}_{1}\right\}

(134)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}2^{-n\frac{\eta}{10}}\right\}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\norm{\rho_{X^{n}(m)}}_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle~~~~~+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\norm{ \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi}_{1}\right\}

(135)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle~~~~~+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\mathds{1}_{\{X^{n}(m)\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\norm{ \pi}_{\infty}\norm{\pi_{m}}_{\infty}\norm{\rho_{X^{n}(m)}}_{1}\norm{\pi_{m}}_{\infty}\norm{\pi}_{\infty}\right\}

(136)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!2^{-n\frac{\eta}{10}}+\frac{2}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{u^{n}}\frac{q_{X}^{n}(u^{n})}{p_{X}^{n}(u^{n})}p_{X}(u^{n})\mathds{1}_{\{u^{n}\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\leq 2^{-n\frac{\eta}{10}}+2\cdot 2^{-n\frac{\eta}{20}}

(137)

for sufficiently large
n
, where (i) (
133
) follows from the triangular inequality, (ii) (

and the repeated use of the bound

,

implying that the second and third terms of (

Upper Bound on
T_{3}
: We note that

\displaystyle\norm{\overline{A}-\overline{B}}_{1}\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\norm{\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ A_{t}(m)-B_{t}(m)\right\}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ A_{nt}(m)-B_{nt}(m)\right\}}_{1}

\displaystyle\!\!\leq\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\norm{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ A_{t}(m)-B_{t}(m)\right\}}_{1}+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\norm{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ A_{nt}(m)-B_{nt}(m)\right\}}_{1}

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\norm{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ \frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\left[\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\right] \right\}}_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\norm{\Expectation_{\mathbf{P}}\left\{ \frac{q_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}{p_{X}^{n}(X^{n}(m))}\left[\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{X^{n}(m)}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ X^{n}(m) \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\right] \right\}}_{1}

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\norm{\sum_{u^{n} \in\CalX^{n}} \frac{q_{X}^{n}(u^{n})}{p_{X}^{n}(u^{n})}p_{X}(u^{n})\left[\rho_{u^{n}}\mathds{1}_{\{ u^{n} \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{u^{n}}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ u^{n} \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\right] }_{1}

\displaystyle\!\!\leq\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{u^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}q_{X}(u^{n})\norm{\left[\rho_{u^{n}}\mathds{1}_{\{ u^{n} \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{u^{n}}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ u^{n} \in T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\right] }_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{v^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}q_{X}^{n}(v^{n})\norm{\left[\rho_{v^{n}}\mathds{1}_{\{ v^{n} \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}} - \pi\pi_{m}\rho_{v^{n}}\pi_{m}\pi\mathds{1}_{\{ v^{n} \notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\right] }_{1}

(139)

\displaystyle\!\!\leq\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{u^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}q_{X}(u^{n})2^{-n\frac{\eta}{10}}++\frac{2}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{v^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}}q_{X}^{n}(v^{n})\mathds{1}_{\{v^{n}\notin T_{{\eta}}^{n}(q_{X})\}}\leq 2^{-n\frac{\eta}{5}}

(140)

for sufficiently large
n
, where (i) (
138
), (
139
) follow from triangular inequality and (ii) (
140
) follows from (
132
) and

Here, the last inequality above follows from the same argument we provided in going from (

) to (
137
).

The proof of the theorem follows by collating through the bounds derived in (

), (

∎

Appendix D
Simulation Via Algebraically Closed POVMs

We briefly revisit the simulated POVM in proof of Thm.
2
. Alice possesses POVM operators
\theta=\{\overline{\theta}_{k,m}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\theta_{k,m}\otimes\outerproduct{k}{k}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[\overline{M}]\}
and Bob has a corresponding table
c=\{w^{n}(k,m):(k,m)\in[K]\times[{M}]\}
. On observing (common) random bits
K=k^{*}
, Alice performs POVM
\{\theta_{k^{*},m}:0\leq m\leq M\}
. The outcome
M=m^{*}
is communicated to Bob who chooses
w^{n}(k^{*},m^{*})
and evolves this through the stochastic matrix
p_{Y|W}^{n}(\cdot|w^{n}(k^{*},m^{*}))
. The question we ask in this section is : can the table
c
, and the corresponding POVMs
\theta
be endowed with certain
algebraic
structure? Specifically, suppose
\mathcal{W}
is a finite field with
\oplus
denoting addition in
\mathcal{W}
, can we build a likelihood POVM based on a table
\{w^{n}(k,m)\in\mathcal{W}^{n}:(k,m)\in[K]\times[{M}]\}
that satisfies
w^{n}(k_{1},m_{1})\oplus w^{n}(k_{2},m_{2})=(w(k_{1},m_{1})_{1}\oplus w(k_{2},m_{2})_{1},\cdots,w(k_{1},m_{1})_{n}\oplus w(k_{2},m_{2})_{n})\in c
for every choice of
k_{1},m_{1},k_{2},m_{2}
, where
\oplus
denotes addition in the field
\mathcal{W}
?. Will they be able to simulate the action of
\lambda
on
\rho
with minimum communication rate with a likelihood POVM whose table satisfies the above mentioned property?

Optimal compression requires that each outcome
w^{n}(k,m)
is an equally likely POVM outcome, forcing the entries of the table to be
p_{W}-
typical, where, we recall
p_{W}(w)=\tr(\rho\mu_{w})
. Requiring table entries to be algebraically closed forces us to choose entries
w^{n}(k,m)
that are
not

p_{W}-
typical. Indeed, a collection of words that are all
p_{W}-
typical need not be algebraically closed. However, non-
p_{w}-
typical outcomes are extremely rare lending the simulation protocol sub-optimal in terms of communication rates.
Does this imply that the table cannot possess algebraic properties?

We overcome this obstacle by leveraging the information-theoretic idea of
binning
and enlarging the table into a third dimension. We refer the reader back to proof of Thm.
2
and replace the code therein with the code
\hat{c}:[K]\times[M]\times[B]\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n}
and the POVM therein with POVM
\hat{\theta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\hat{\theta}_{k,m,b}\otimes\outerproduct{k}{k}:(k,m,b)\in[K]\times[M]\times[B]\}
. On observing random bits
k^{*}
, Alice performs POVM
\hat{\theta}_{k^{*}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\hat{\theta}_{k^{*},m,b}:1\leq m\leq M,1\leq b\leq B\}
. Only the component
m^{*}
of the outcome
(m^{*},b^{*})
is communicated to Bob. If the table has the desired property that for each
(k,m)\in[K]\times[M]
, there is a
unique
index
b^{*}_{k,m}\in[B]
such that
w^{n}(k,m,b^{*}_{k,m})
is
p_{W}-
typical, then Bob can evolve
w^{n}(k^{*},m^{*},b^{*}_{k,m})
through the stochastic matrix
p^{n}_{Y|W}(\cdot|w^{n}(k^{*},m^{*},b^{*}_{k,m}))
and simulate the POVM outcome. Since the POVM outcome is
p_{W}-
typical with high probability, Bob’s choice would indeed be the correct POVM outcome with high probability. In this section, we prove that this technique enables build likelihood POVMs to simulate the action of
\lambda^{\otimes}
on
\rho^{\otimes n}
whose corresponding collection
\hat{c}
is algebraically closed and achieves minimum communication costs. We now state the notation employed in the rest of this section.

Remark 7
.

A reader familiar with linear coding theory (LCT) will recognize that we are seeking the table
c
to be a coset of a linear code. We tweak our notation slightly and incorporate objects, language and notation from LCT to describe our results in this section. Unless otherwise stated
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
is a finite field of size
q
with
\oplus
denoting addition in
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
. We use
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}_{q}
interchangeably. In LCT, a coset code with
q^{\kappa}
codewords is defined through a generator matrix
g\in\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\kappa\times n}
and a bias vector
d^{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{q}^{n}
. The codewords of this code are
(a^{\kappa}g\oplus d^{n}:a^{\kappa}\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa})
. Towards leveraging this notation, we make the following changes. We let shared randomness to be uniformly distributed over
\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}
. Henceforth,
K=q^{\kappa}
,
M=q^{r}
,
B=q^{\beta}
and we identify
[K]
with
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\kappa}
,
[M]
with
\mathcal{F}_{q}^{r}
,
B=q^{\beta}
i.e,
[K]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\kappa}
,
[M]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{r}
and
[B]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\beta}
. We also let
\underline{k}=(k_{1},\cdots,k_{\kappa})\in[K]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\kappa}
denote a generic realization of the shared random bits. Similarly, we let
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{r})\in[M]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{r}
denote a generic realization of the message communicated by Alice over the communication link and
\underline{b}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{\beta})\in[B]=\mathcal{F}_{q}^{\beta}
denote a generic index in this set. The table
c:[K]\times[M]\times[B]\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n}
is defined via generator matrices
g_{c}\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa\times n}
,
g_{m}\in\mathcal{W}^{r\times n}
,
g_{b}\in\mathcal{W}^{\beta\times n}
and bias word
d^{n}\in\mathcal{W}^{n}
and
c(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})=\underline{k}g_{c}\oplus\underline{m}g_{m}\oplus\underline{b}g_{b}\oplus d^{n}
. In the following definition, we formalize the notion of an algebraically closed likelihood POVM.

Definition 8
.

Given
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
, a POVM
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}=\{\mu_{w}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}):w\in\mathcal{W}\}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{W})
with
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
a finite field of size
q
, a map
c:\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n}
with
w^{n}(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})=(w(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})_{1},\cdots,w(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})_{n})=c(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})
and a non-negative valued function
\upsilon:\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, the
(n,\rho,\mu_{\mathcal{W}},c,\upsilon)-
likelihood POVM is defined as the POVM
\theta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\overline{\theta}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\otimes\outerproduct{\ulinek}{\ulinek}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}}):(\underline{k},\underline{m})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r},\theta_{\underline{k},0}\}
where

\displaystyle\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}=\sum_{\underline{b}}\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}},~{\theta}_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\upsilon({\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}})\frac{{S}_{\underline{k}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\omega}\mu_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}}\sqrt{\omega}{S}_{\underline{k}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\tr(\omega\mu_{\ulinek,\ulm,\ulb})},~\mu_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigotimes_{t=1}^{n}\mu_{w(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})_{t}},

(141)

\displaystyle{S}_{\underline{k}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\!\sum_{\underline{b}}\sum_{\underline{m}}\!{\upsilon({\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}})}\frac{\sqrt{\omega}\mu_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}}\sqrt{\omega}}{\tr(\omega\mu_{\ulinek,\ulm,\ulb})},~~~\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho^{\otimes n}\mbox{ and }\theta_{\underline{k},0}=I_{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}-\sum_{\underline{m}}\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}=I_{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}-z_{k}

(142)

where
z_{k}
is the projector on the support of
S_{\underline{k}}
.
14
14

14

In the definition of
{\theta}_{\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}}
,
\frac{\boldsymbol{0}}{0}
is defined as
\boldsymbol{0}
, where
\boldsymbol{0}
denotes the zero operator.
We say the
(n,\rho,\mu_{\mathcal{W}},c)-
likelihood POVM has parameters
(\frac{\kappa\log q}{n},\frac{r\log q}{n},\frac{\beta\log q}{n})
and communication cost
(\frac{\kappa\log q}{n},\frac{r\log q}{n})
.

We say
(n,\rho,\mu_{\mathcal{W}},c)-
likelihood POVM is algebraically closed if there exists generator matrices
g_{c}\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa\times n}
,
g_{m}\in\mathcal{W}^{r\times n}
,
g_{b}\in\mathcal{W}^{\beta\times n}
and a bias vector
d^{n}\in\mathcal{W}^{n}
such that
c(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})=\underline{k}g_{c}\oplus\underline{m}g_{m}\oplus\underline{b}g_{b}\oplus d^{n}
for
(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}
.

Definition 9
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{Y})
be a POVM. POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho
can be simulated with communication costs
(C,R)
via an algebraically closed likelihood POVM if for every
\eta>0
, there exists
N_{\eta}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N_{\eta}
, there exists an algebraically closed
(n,\rho,\mu_{\mathcal{W}},c)-
likelihood POVM
\theta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\overline{\theta}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\otimes\outerproduct{\ulinek}{\ulinek}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{\kappa}}):(\underline{k},\underline{m})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r},\theta_{\underline{k},0}\}
defined via (
141
) where
[B]=\mathcal{W}_{\beta},B=q^{\beta}
and a decoding POVM
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{H}_{q^{\kappa}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q^{r}},\mathcal{Y}^{n})
such that
\frac{\kappa\log q}{n}\leq C+\eta
,
\frac{r\log q}{n}\leq R+\eta
and
||\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny O}}-\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny S}}||_{1}\leq\eta
, where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny O}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}^{n}\!\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\lambda^{n}}\!)(\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho^{\otimes n}}}{\phi_{\rho^{\otimes n}}}),\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny S}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{E}_{\mbox{\tiny sim}}}}(\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho_{\ulineK}^{\otimes n}}\!\!}{\!\phi_{\rho_{\ulineK}^{\otimes n}}\!}),\!\!\!

(143)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{E}_{\mbox{\tiny sim}}}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}^{n}\!\otimes\!\tr_{\underline{K}}\!\otimes i_{\mathcal{Y}^{n}})\circ(i_{X}^{n}\!\otimes\!i_{\mathcal{H}_{\underline{K}}}\!\otimes\!\mathscr{E}^{\Delta}\!)\!\circ\!(i_{X}^{n}\!\otimes\!i_{\mathcal{H}_{\underline{K}}}\!\otimes\!\mathscr{E}^{\theta}),\!\!\!

(144)

i_{X}^{n}
abbreviates the identity map
i_{X}^{\otimes n}
on
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{X}^{\otimes n})
.

If POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho
can be simulated with communication costs
(C,R)
via an algebraically closed likelihood POVM for any
(C,R)\in\mathscr{C}\mathscr{R}(\rho,\Lambda_{\mathcal{Y}})
, we say POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho
can be simulated via algebraically closed likelihood POVMs.

We now state the main result of this section.

Theorem 6
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{Y})
be a POVM. POVM
\lambda_{\mathcal{Y}}
on
\rho
can be simulated via algebraically closed likelihood POVMs.

D-A

Proof of Theorem
6

Preliminaries and Simulation POVM
: Given
(C,R)\in\mathscr{C}\mathscr{R}(\rho,\lambda)
, our goal is to specify
(n,C,R,\theta,\Delta)
simulation protocol for which
\norm{\alphaorig-\alphasim}_{1}
shrinks exponentially in
n
, where
\theta
is an algebraically closed likelihood POVM, and
\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny O}},\alpha_{\mbox{\tiny S}}
are as defined in (
143
), (
144
). Let
({\mathcal{W}},\mu_{{\mathcal{W}}},p_{Y|{W}})
be such that
C+R>I(XY;{W})_{\sigma}
and
R>I(X;{W})_{\sigma}
where
\sigma_{X{W}Y}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(i_{X}\otimes\overline{\mathscr{E}}^{{Y|{W}}}_{p})\circ(i_{X}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\mu})(\outerproduct{\phi_{\rho}}{\phi_{\rho}})
. We enlarge
{\mathcal{W}}
to a finite field
\mathcal{F}_{q}
by adding dummy symbols. Each added dummy symbol is associated with the zero operator and the corresponding rows of the stochastic matrix
p_{Y|W}
can be chosen arbitrarily. Thus, we now have
(\mathcal{W},\mu_{\mathcal{W}},p_{Y|W})
such that
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{F}_{q}
is a finite field and
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}=\{\mu_{w}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}):w\in\mathcal{W}\}
is a POVM for which the above stated bounds on
C,R
hold.

Let
\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\rho^{\otimes n}
. Let
g_{c}\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa\times n}
,
g_{m}\in\mathcal{W}^{r\times n}
and
g_{b}\in\mathcal{W}^{\beta\times n}
be generator matrices and
d^{n}\in\mathcal{W}^{n}
be a bias vector. Let
c:\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n}
be a map with
c(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}w^{n}(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(w(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})_{1},\cdots,w(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})_{n})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\underline{k}g_{c}\oplus\underline{m}g_{m}\oplus\underline{b}g_{b}\oplus d^{n}
for
(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}
. Let
\upsilon:\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{\beta}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is defined as
\upsilon(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})={q^{n}\tr(\mu_{\ulinek,\ulinem,\ulineb}\omega)}
. Consider the
(n,\rho,\mu_{\mathcal{W}},c,\upsilon)
-likelihood POVM
\theta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\overline{\theta}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\theta_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\otimes\outerproduct{\ulinek}{\ulinek}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}}):(\underline{k},\underline{m})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r},\theta_{\underline{k},0}\}
as defined in (
141
). It is straightforward to verify
\theta
is a valid POVM. This can be done by mimicking the steps in proof of Thm.
2
. For every
(\underline{k},\underline{m})\in\mathcal{W}^{\kappa}\times\mathcal{W}^{r}
, we let

\displaystyle\mathrm{l}(\underline{k},\underline{m})=\sum_{\underline{b}\in\mathcal{W}^{\beta}}\mathds{1}_{\{w^{n}(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\in T_{\eta}(p_{W})\}},\mbox{ where }p_{W}(w)=\tr(\rho\mu_{w}),

be the number of
p_{W}-
typical elements in the coset corresponding to the index
(\underline{k},\underline{m})
. We define

\displaystyle\underline{b}^{*}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\begin{cases}\underline{b}&\mbox{if }\mathrm{l}(\underline{k},\underline{m})=1\mbox{ and }w^{n}(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b})\mbox{ is the unique element that is }p_{W}-\mbox{typical},\\
\underline{0}&\mbox{if }\mathrm{l}(\underline{k},\underline{m})\neq 1.\end{cases}

In other words,
\underline{b}^{*}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}
is the index of the unique element that is
p_{W}-
typical, if exactly one element is
p_{W}-
typical. Otherwise,
b^{*}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}}
is assigned
\underline{0}
. Next, we set

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\Delta_{y^{n}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-3.28473pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-3.28473pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sum_{(\underline{k},\underline{m})\in[K]\times[M]}\outerproduct{\ulk~ \ulm}{\ulk~ \ulm}p_{Y|W}^{n}(y^{n}|c(\underline{k},\underline{m},\underline{b}^{*}_{\underline{k},\underline{m}})):y^{n}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}\}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{H}_{K}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M},\mathcal{Y}^{n})

(145)

as the post-processing POVM employed by the decoder. It is straight forward to verify
